\displaystyle=\underbrace{\frac{\gamma_{m}}{\gamma_{p}}\frac{D\cdot\Im(\varepsilon_{p})}{2\pi\kappa_{m}}f(t)\int_{D}|\mathrm{E}|^{2}\,d\mathrm{y}}_{:=\text{Dominant Term}}+\underbrace{\frac{\gamma_{m}}{\gamma_{p}}\frac{1}{\kappa_{m}}\int_{D}\partial_{t}\bm{\mathbcal{V}^{t}}_{D}\big[\mathrm{J}\big](x,t)\,dx}_{:=\textbf{err}^{(3)}}+\textbf{err}^{(1)}+\textbf{err}^{(2)}.

(2.49)

We now focus on estimating the term
\textbf{err}^{(3)}
. First, since
\mathrm{J}(x,t)
satisfies
\mathrm{J}(\cdot,0)=0
identically, and referring to
[
12
, Theorem 5]
, the fundamental solution
\Phi^{(m)}(x,t;y,\tau)
satisfies
\big(\partial_{t}-\Delta\big)\Phi^{(m)}(x,t;y,\tau)=\delta_{0}(x,y)
, then by differentiating
\bm{\mathbcal{V}^{t}}_{D}\big[\mathrm{J}\big]
with respect to time and using integration by parts, it follows that

\partial_{t}\bm{\mathbcal{V}^{t}}_{D}\big[\mathrm{J}\big]=\bm{\mathbcal{V}^{t}}_{D}\big[\partial_{t}\mathrm{J}\big].

Thus, we can rewrite the above expression as

\displaystyle\bm{\mathbcal{V}^{t}}_{D}\big[\partial_{t}\mathrm{J}\big]

\displaystyle=\int_{D}\frac{1}{4\pi|x-y|}\partial_{t}\mathrm{J}(y,\tau)\,dy+\int_{D}\frac{1}{4\pi|x-y|}\left(\varphi(x,y,t)-\partial_{t}\mathrm{J}(y,t)\right)\,dy.

(2.50)

To estimate the second term in the above equation, we refer to the following lemma. The justification follows similarly to the proof of Lemma 3.2 in
[
33
]
, and thus we omit it here.

Lemma 2.2
.

We set
\varphi(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y},\mathrm{t})
as follows:

\varphi(x,\mathrm{y},\mathrm{t}):=\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\kappa_{m}^{\frac{3}{2}}|x-y|}{2\sqrt{\pi}(t-\tau)^{\frac{3}{2}}}\textbf{exp}\big(-\dfrac{|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|^{2}}{4(\mathrm{t}-\tau)}\big)f(y,\tau)d\tau.

Then we have

\varphi(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y},\mathrm{t})-f(y,\tau)=\mathcal{O}\bigg(|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|\ \|\partial_{\mathrm{t}}f(y,\cdot)\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,t)}\bigg),

for
\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y}
such that
|\mathrm{v}-\mathrm{y}|\ll\mathrm{t}
and
\mathrm{t}\in(0,\mathrm{T}]
uniformly with respect to
D
.

Taking the integral with respect to
D
to the expression (
2.50
), we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{D}\bm{\mathbcal{V}^{t}}_{D}\big[\partial_{t}\mathrm{J}\big]

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\int_{D}\int_{D}\frac{1}{4\pi|x-y|}\mathrm{J}(y,\tau)\,dy\,dx}_{:=\textbf{err}^{(4)}}+\underbrace{\int_{D}\int_{D}\frac{1}{4\pi|x-y|}\left(\varphi(x,y,t)-\mathrm{J}(y,t)\right)\,dy\,dx}_{:=\textbf{err}^{(5)}}.

(2.51)

Then, we deduce

\displaystyle\textbf{err}^{(4)}:=\Big|\int_{D}\int_{D}\frac{1}{4\pi|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|}\partial_{t}J(y,\tau)dy\Big|

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\partial_{t}f(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\Big(\int_{D\times D}\frac{1}{4\pi|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|^{2}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\ |D|^{\frac{1}{2}}\ \Im(\varepsilon_{p})\ \big\|\mathrm{E}\big\|^{2}_{L^{4}(D)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\Big(|D|\sup\limits_{y\in\partial D}\int_{D}\dfrac{dx}{|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|^{2}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\ |D|^{\frac{1}{2}}\ \Im(\varepsilon_{p})\ \big\|\mathrm{E}\big\|^{2}_{L^{4}(D)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\delta^{\frac{9}{2}-h}.

(2.52)

Again,

\displaystyle\textbf{err}^{(4)}:=\Big|\int_{D}\int_{D}\frac{1}{4\pi|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|}\Big(\varphi(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y},\mathrm{t})-\partial_{t}J(y,t)\Big)dy\Big|

\displaystyle\lesssim|D\times D|^{\frac{1}{2}}\ |D|^{\frac{1}{2}}\ \|\partial^{2}_{t}\mathrm{J}(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{2}\big(D\big)}

\displaystyle\lesssim|D\times D|^{\frac{1}{2}}\ |D|^{\frac{1}{2}}\ \|\partial^{2}_{t}f(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\ \Im(\varepsilon_{p})\big\|\mathrm{E}\big\|^{2}_{L^{4}(D)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\delta^{6-h}.

(2.53)

Therefore, combining the estimates (
2.52
), (
2.53
), and plugging these into (
2.51
), we deduce that

\displaystyle\textbf{err}^{(3)}:=\Bigg|\frac{\gamma_{m}}{\gamma_{p}}\frac{1}{\kappa_{m}}\int_{D}\partial_{t}\bm{\mathbcal{V}^{t}}_{D}\big[\mathrm{J}\big](x,t)\ dx\Bigg|\lesssim\delta^{\frac{9}{2}+\beta-h}.

(2.54)

Recall that if
D
is a bounded open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
of class
\mathcal{C}^{1,\alpha}
with
\alpha\in(0,1]
, then there exists a constant
c_{D,\alpha}>0
such that

|(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{v})\cdot\nu_{\mathbf{v}}|\leq c_{D,\alpha}|\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{v}|^{1+\alpha}.

Consequently, using the a priori estimate for
\|\partial_{\nu}u(y,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}(\partial D)}\sim\delta^{1+\beta-h}
and noting that
\alpha\sim\delta^{-\beta}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\textbf{err}^{(1)}:=\mathcal{O}\left(\alpha\int_{\partial D}\int_{\partial D}\frac{\langle\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}\cdot\nu_{\mathbf{y}}\rangle}{4\pi|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|^{3}}\frac{|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|^{3}}{t^{3/2}}\partial_{\nu}u(y,\tau)\,d\sigma_{y}\right)=\mathcal{O}(\delta^{6-h}).

(2.55)

Next, we focus on estimating the term
\textbf{err}^{(2)}
. We have

\displaystyle\textbf{err}^{(2)}

\displaystyle:=\vartheta\int_{\partial D}\partial_{\nu}\bm{\mathbcal{V}^{t}}_{D}\left[\partial_{t}u\right](x,t)\,d\sigma_{x}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|1\|_{L^{2}(\partial D)}\|\partial_{\nu}\bm{\mathbcal{V}^{t}}_{D}\left[\partial_{t}u\right]\|_{L^{2}(\partial D)}

(2.56)

We follow a similar approach as we did to estimate in (
2.54
) to deduce

\displaystyle\partial_{\nu_{x}}\bm{\mathbcal{V}^{t}}_{D}\left[\partial_{t}u\right](x,t)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{D}\partial_{\nu_{x}}\Phi^{(m)}(x,t;y,\tau)\,\partial_{t}u(y,\tau)\,d\tau

\displaystyle=\int_{D}\frac{\langle\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x}\cdot\nu_{\mathbf{x}}\rangle}{4\pi|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|^{3}}\left[\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|^{3}}{(t-\tau)^{3/2}}\frac{1}{2(t-\tau)}e^{-\frac{|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|^{2}}{4(\tau-\mathrm{s})}}\,d\tau\right]d\sigma_{\mathbf{y}}.

We define

\varphi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},t):=\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi}(t-\tau)^{3/2}}\frac{|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|^{3}}{(t-\tau)}e^{-\frac{|\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{v}|^{2}}{4(\mathrm{s}-\tau)}}u(y,\tau)\,d\tau,

from which we deduce

\displaystyle\partial_{\nu_{x}}\bm{\mathbcal{V}^{t}}_{D}\left[\partial_{t}u\right](x,t)

\displaystyle=\int_{D}\frac{\langle\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x}\cdot\nu_{\mathbf{x}}\rangle}{4\pi|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|^{3}}\partial_{t}u(y,\tau)\,dy+\int_{D}\frac{\langle\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x}\cdot\nu_{\mathbf{x}}\rangle}{4\pi|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|^{3}}\left(\varphi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},t)-\partial_{t}u(y,t)\right)\,dy.

(2.57)

Integrating with respect to
\partial D
, we obtain

\displaystyle\|\partial_{\nu}\bm{\mathbcal{V}^{t}}_{D}\left[\partial_{t}u\right]\|_{L^{2}(\partial D)}

\displaystyle=\Bigg(\int_{\partial D}\Big|\partial_{\nu_{x}}\bm{\mathbcal{V}^{t}}_{D}\left[\partial_{t}u\right](x,t)\Big|^{2}\Bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\Bigg(\int_{\partial D}\Big|\int_{D}\frac{\langle\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x}\cdot\nu_{\mathbf{x}}\rangle}{4\pi|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|^{3}}\partial_{t}u(y,\tau)\,dy\Big|^{2}\Bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}}_{:=\text{err}^{(6)}}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\Bigg(\int_{\partial D}\Big|\int_{D}\frac{\langle\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x}\cdot\nu_{\mathbf{x}}\rangle}{4\pi|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|^{3}}\left(\varphi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},t)-\partial_{t}u(y,t)\right)\,dy\Big|^{2}\Bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}}_{:=\text{err}^{(7)}}.

(2.58)

To estimate the second term in the equation above, we state the following lemma.

Lemma 2.3
.

Define
\varphi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},t)
as follows:

\varphi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},t):=\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi}(t-\tau)^{3/2}}\frac{|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|^{3}}{(t-\tau)}e^{-\frac{|\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{v}|^{2}}{4(\mathrm{s}-\tau)}}u(y,\tau)\,d\tau.

Then

\varphi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},t)-u(y,t)=\mathcal{O}\left(|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|\,\|\partial_{t}u(y,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}(0,t)}\right),

for
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}
such that
|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|\ll t
and
t\in(0,T]
, uniformly with respect to
D
.

Proof.

We start by considering the change of variable
\mathrm{m}:=\frac{|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|}{2\sqrt{t-\tau}}
. Then it follows that
\tau=t-\frac{|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|^{2}}{4\mathrm{m}^{2}}
. Differentiating
\tau
with respect to
m
, we obtain:

d\tau=\frac{|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|^{2}}{2}\mathrm{m}^{-3}\,dm.

Therefore, we deduce

\displaystyle\varphi(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y},\mathrm{t})=\frac{4}{\sqrt{\pi}}\displaystyle\int_{\frac{|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|}{2\sqrt{t}}}^{\infty}\mathrm{m}^{2}e^{-\mathrm{m}^{2}}\ u\big(y,\mathrm{t}-\frac{|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|^{2}}{4\mathrm{m}^{2}}\big)d\mathrm{m},

which, we rewrite as

\displaystyle\varphi(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y},\mathrm{t})=\frac{4}{\sqrt{\pi}}\displaystyle\int_{\frac{|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|}{2\sqrt{t}}}^{\infty}\mathrm{m}^{2}e^{-\mathrm{m}^{2}}\ \Big(u\big(y,\mathrm{t}-\frac{|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|^{2}}{4\mathrm{m}^{2}}\big)-u(y,t)\Big)dm+\frac{4}{\sqrt{\pi}}u(y,t)\displaystyle\int_{\frac{|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|}{2\sqrt{t}}}^{\infty}\mathrm{m}^{2}e^{-\mathrm{m}^{2}}\ dm

We now use the fact that
\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{m}^{2}\textbf{exp}(-\mathrm{m}^{2})d\mathrm{m}=\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{4}
to obtain

\displaystyle\varphi(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y},\mathrm{t})-u(y,t)=\frac{4}{\sqrt{\pi}}\displaystyle\int_{\frac{|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|}{2\sqrt{t}}}^{\infty}\mathrm{m}^{2}e^{-\mathrm{m}^{2}}\ \Big(u\big(y,\mathrm{t}-\frac{|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|^{2}}{4\mathrm{m}^{2}}\big)-u(y,t)\Big)dm-\frac{4}{\sqrt{\pi}}u(y,t)\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\frac{|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|}{2\sqrt{t}}}\mathrm{m}^{2}e^{-\mathrm{m}^{2}}\ dm

(2.59)

Considering
u(y,0)=0,
we have
\displaystyle\partial_{t}u=\int_{0}^{t}\partial_{t}(y,s)ds
and then by using Cauchy-Schwartz inequality, we deduce the following estimate

\displaystyle u(y,t)=\mathcal{O}\Big(t^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\partial_{t}u(y,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}(0,t)}\Big)

We now observe that
\mathrm{m}\geq\frac{\sqrt{\alpha}|\mathrm{y}-\mathrm{v}|}{2\sqrt{t}}
which implies
t-\frac{|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|^{2}}{4\mathrm{m}^{2}}\geq 0
. Consequently, we derive the similar estimate as before

\displaystyle u\big(y,\mathrm{t}-\frac{|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|^{2}}{4\mathrm{m}^{2}}\big)-u(y,t)

\displaystyle=\int_{t}^{\frac{|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|^{2}}{4m^{2}}}\partial_{t}u(y,s)ds

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\Big(\frac{|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|^{2}}{4\mathrm{m}^{2}}\|\partial_{t}u(y,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}(0,t)}\Big)

Now, plugging the above two estimate in (
2.59
) to obtain

\displaystyle\varphi(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y},\mathrm{t})-u(y,t)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\bigg(\frac{4}{\sqrt{\pi}}\displaystyle\int_{\frac{|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|}{2\sqrt{t}}}^{\infty}\mathrm{m}^{2}\bm{\mathrm{e}}^{-\mathrm{m}^{2}}d\mathrm{m}\frac{|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|^{2}}{4m^{2}}\|\partial_{t}u(y,\cdot)\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,t)}\bigg)

\displaystyle+\mathcal{O}\bigg(\frac{4}{\sqrt{\pi}}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\frac{|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|}{2\sqrt{t}}}\mathrm{m}^{2}\bm{\mathrm{e}}^{-\mathrm{m}^{2}}d\mathrm{m}\;t^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\partial_{t}u(y,\cdot)\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,t)}\bigg).

(2.60)

Therefore, considering the regime
|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|\ll\sqrt{t}
, and as we have error function’s Maclaurin series as:
\textbf{erf}(\mathrm{a})=\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\displaystyle\sum_{\mathrm{n=0}}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{\mathrm{n}}\mathrm{a}^{2\mathrm{n}+1}}{(2\mathrm{n}+1)\mathrm{n}!},
we deduce

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\frac{|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|}{2\sqrt{t}}}\mathrm{m}^{2}\bm{\mathrm{e}}^{-\mathrm{m}^{2}}d\mathrm{m}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{4}\ \textbf{erf}\Big(\frac{|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|}{2\sqrt{t}}\Big)-\frac{|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|}{4\sqrt{t}}\ e^{-}{\dfrac{|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|^{2}}{4t}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\Big(\frac{1}{t^{\frac{3}{2}}}|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|^{3}\Big).

(2.61)

Similarly, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\frac{|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|}{2\sqrt{t}}}^{\infty}\bm{\mathrm{e}}^{-\mathrm{m}^{2}}d\mathrm{m}=\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{2}-\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{2}\textbf{erf}\Big(\frac{|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|}{2\sqrt{t}}\Big).

(2.62)

Then, if we plug the previous two estimates (
2.61
) and (
2.62
) in (
2.60
), we deduce

\varphi(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y},\mathrm{t})-u(y,t)=\mathcal{O}\bigg(|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|\ \|\partial_{\mathrm{t}}u(y,\cdot)\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,t)}\bigg),

(2.63)

for
\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y}
such that
|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|\ll\mathrm{t}
and
\mathrm{t}\in(0,\mathrm{T}]
uniformly with respect to
D
. The proof is complete.
∎

Given that
D
is a bounded open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
of class
\mathcal{C}^{1,\alpha}
with
\alpha\in(0,1]
, there exists a constant
c_{D,\alpha}>0
such that

|(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{v})\cdot\nu_{\mathbf{v}}|\leq c_{D,\alpha}|\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{v}|^{1+\alpha}.

Thus,

\displaystyle\Bigg(\int_{\partial D}\Big|\int_{D}\frac{\langle\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x}\cdot\nu_{\mathbf{x}}\rangle}{4\pi|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|^{3}}\partial_{t}u(y,\tau)\,dy\Big|^{2}\Bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\int_{\partial D\times D}\frac{|\langle\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x}\cdot\nu_{\mathbf{x}}\rangle|^{2}}{4\pi|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|^{6}}\right)^{1/2}|\partial D|^{1/2}\|\partial_{t}u(y,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}(D)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\int_{\partial D\times D}\frac{1}{|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|^{2}}\,dx\,dy\right)^{1/2}|\partial D|^{1/2}\|\partial_{t}u(y,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}(D)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\delta^{3/2}\left(\int_{\partial B\times B}\frac{1}{|\xi-\eta|^{2}}\,d\xi\,d\eta\right)^{1/2}|\partial D|^{1/2}\|\partial_{t}u(y,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}(D)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\delta^{5-h}.

(2.64)

Similarly,

\displaystyle\Bigg(\int_{\partial D}\Big|\int_{D}\frac{\langle\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x}\cdot\nu_{\mathbf{x}}\rangle}{4\pi|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|^{3}}\left(\varphi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},t)-\partial_{t}u(y,t)\right)\,dy\Big|^{2}\Bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\int_{\partial D\times D}\frac{|\langle\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x}\cdot\nu_{\mathbf{x}}\rangle|^{2}}{4\pi|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|^{4}}\right)^{1/2}|\partial D|^{1/2}\|\partial^{2}_{t}u(y,t)\|_{L^{2}(D\times(0,t))}

\displaystyle\lesssim|\partial D\times D|^{1/2}|\partial D|^{1/2}\int_{0}^{t}\|\partial^{2}_{t}u(y,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}(D)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\delta^{6-h}.

(2.65)

Thus, using the estimates (
2.64
), (
2.65
), and substituting them into (
2.56
), we obtain

\displaystyle\textbf{err}^{(2)}:=\vartheta\int_{\partial D}\partial_{\nu}\bm{\mathbcal{V}^{t}}_{D}\left[\partial_{t}u\right](x,t)\,d\sigma_{x}\lesssim\delta^{6-h}.

(2.66)

Therefore, combining (
2.54
), (
2.55
), and (
2.66
) in (
2.49
), we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\partial D}\partial_{\nu}u

\displaystyle=\frac{\gamma_{m}}{\gamma_{p}}\frac{1}{\kappa_{m}}\frac{D\cdot\Im(\varepsilon_{p})}{2\pi}\int_{D}|\mathbf{E}|^{2}\,d\mathbf{y}+\mathcal{O}(\delta^{6-h}).

(2.67)

This estimate will be used in the following section.

2.3.1
End of the Proof of Theorem
1.1
: The Asymptotic Expansions

We now present the asymptotic expansion of the solution to (
1.4
) as
\delta\to 0
, based on the integral representation

\displaystyle u(x,t)

\displaystyle+\vartheta\frac{1}{\kappa_{m}}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{D}\Phi^{(m)}(x,t;y,\tau)\ \partial_{t}u(y,\tau)\ dyd\tau+\alpha\frac{1}{\kappa_{m}}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\partial D}\Phi^{(m)}(x,t;y,\tau)\ \partial_{\nu}u(y,\tau)\ d\sigma_{y}d\tau

\displaystyle=\frac{\gamma_{m}}{\gamma_{p}}\frac{k\cdot\Im(\varepsilon_{p})}{2\pi}\ \frac{1}{\kappa_{m}}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{D}\Phi^{(m)}(x,t;y,\tau)|\mathrm{E}|^{2}(y)\ f(\tau)dyd\tau.

(2.68)

Observe that for any fixed
(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\overline{D}\times(0,T)
, the function
\Phi^{(m)}(x,t;y,\tau)
is sufficiently smooth with respect to
(y,\tau)\in\partial D\times(0,t)
. Consequently, the application of Taylor’s series expansion yields

\big|\Phi^{(m)}(x,t;y,\tau)-\Phi^{(m)}(x,t;z,\tau)\big|\lesssim\delta,

for
z\in D
,
y\in\partial D
, and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\overline{D}.

\displaystyle u(x,t)=-\alpha\frac{1}{\kappa_{m}}\int_{0}^{t}\Phi^{(m)}(x,t;z,\tau)\Big(\int_{\partial D}\partial_{\nu}u(y,\tau)d\sigma_{y}\Big)d\tau+\text{error},

(2.69)

where
\text{error}:=\text{err}^{(1)}+\text{err}^{(2)}+\text{err}^{(3)}
. Using the same notation as in
[
33
]
, consider a nanoparticle occupying a domain
D=\delta\mathrm{B}+\mathrm{z}
, where
\mathrm{B}
is centered at the origin and
|\mathrm{B}|\sim 1.
Moreover, we use the following notation for defining functions
\varphi
and
\psi
on
\partial D\times(0,T)
and
\partial\mathrm{B}\times(0,T)
, respectively:

\hat{\varphi}(\eta,\tilde{\tau})=\varphi^{\Lambda}(\eta,\tilde{\tau}):=\varphi(\delta\eta+\mathrm{z},\delta^{2}\tilde{\tau}),\quad\quad\quad\check{\psi}(\mathrm{x},t)=\psi^{\vee}(\mathrm{x},t):=\psi\left(\frac{\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{z}}{\delta},\frac{t}{\delta^{2}}\right)

(2.70)

for
(\mathrm{x},t)\in\partial D\times(0,T)
and
(\eta,\tilde{\tau})\in\partial B\times(0,T)
, respectively.

Now, we have

\displaystyle\text{err}^{(1)}

\displaystyle:=\Big|\alpha\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\partial D}\Big(\Phi^{(m)}(x,t;y,\tau)-\Phi^{(m)}(x,t;z,\tau)\Big)\partial_{\nu}u(y,\tau)\ d\sigma_{y}d\tau\Big|

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathcal{O}\Big(\alpha\delta\|1\|_{L^{2}(\partial D\times(0,T))}\|\partial_{\nu}u(y,t)\|_{L^{2}(\partial D\times(0,T))}\Big)

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathcal{O}\Big(\alpha\delta\|1\|_{L^{2}(\partial D\times(0,T))}\int_{0}^{t}\underbrace{\|\partial_{\nu}u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{2}(\partial D)}}_{\delta^{1+\beta-h}}\Big)\lesssim\delta^{4-h}.

(2.71)

Similarly, we rewrite the second term as

\displaystyle\text{err}^{(2)}

\displaystyle:=\Big|\vartheta\bm{\mathbcal{V}^{t}}_{D}\Big[\partial_{t}u\Big](x,t)\Big|

\displaystyle\lesssim\Big|\int_{D}\frac{1}{4\pi|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|}\partial_{t}u(y,\tau)dy\Big|+\Big|\int_{D}\frac{1}{4\pi|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|}\Big(\varphi(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y},\mathrm{t})-\partial_{t}u(y,t)\Big)dy\Big|

\displaystyle\lesssim|D|^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\partial_{t}u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{2}(D)}+|D|^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\partial^{2}_{t}u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{2}(D)}\lesssim\delta^{4-h}.

(2.72)

For the third term, we have

\displaystyle\text{err}^{(3)}

\displaystyle:=\Big|\frac{\gamma_{m}}{\gamma_{p}}\frac{\omega\cdot\Im(\varepsilon_{p})}{2\pi}\bm{\mathbcal{V}^{t}}_{D}\Big[\mathrm{J}\Big](x,t)\Big|

\displaystyle\lesssim\delta^{\beta+h}\Bigg(\Big|\int_{D}\frac{1}{4\pi|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|}\mathrm{J}(y,\tau)dy\Bigg|+\Bigg|\int_{D}\frac{1}{4\pi|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|}\Big(\varphi(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y},\mathrm{t})-\mathrm{J}(y,t)\Big)dy\Big|\Bigg)

\displaystyle\lesssim\delta^{\beta+h}\Bigg(|D|^{\frac{1}{2}}\ \|f(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\big\|\mathrm{E}\big\|^{2}_{L^{4}(D)}+|D|^{\frac{1}{2}}\ \|\partial_{t}f(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\big\|\mathrm{E}\big\|^{2}_{L^{4}(D)}\Bigg)\lesssim\delta^{3+\beta-h}.

(2.73)

Therefore, combining (
2.71
), (
2.72
), and (
2.73
), we derive from (
2.69
) that

\displaystyle u(x,t)=-\alpha\frac{1}{\kappa_{m}}\int_{0}^{t}\Phi^{(m)}(x,t;z,\tau)\Big(\int_{\partial D}\partial_{\nu}u(y,\tau)d\sigma_{y}\Big)d\tau+\mathcal{O}(\delta^{4-h}).

(2.74)

Finally, due to the estimate (
2.67
), we obtain that

\displaystyle u(x,t)=-\frac{k\cdot\Im(\varepsilon_{p})}{2\pi}\frac{\gamma_{m}}{\kappa^{2}_{m}}\int_{0}^{t}\Phi^{(m)}(x,t;z,\tau)f(\tau)d\tau\int_{D}|\mathrm{E}|^{2}(y)\ d\mathrm{y}+\mathcal{O}(\delta^{4-h}).

2.4
Proof of Theorem
1.1
: the Multiple Inclusion Case

Our aim in this section is to provide the asymptotic expansion of the solution to (
1.4
) as
\delta\to 0
for the case when we distribute the multiple nano-particles in the region of interest. Let us now express the solution to (
1.4
) as the following integral equation for
i=1,2,\ldots,M

\displaystyle u^{(i)}(x,t)+\sum_{i=1}^{M}\vartheta_{i}\ \bm{\mathbcal{V}^{t}}_{D_{i}}\big[\partial_{t}u\big](\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t})+\sum_{i=1}^{M}\alpha_{i}\ \bm{\mathcal{S}^{t}}_{\partial D_{i}}\big[\partial_{\nu}u^{(i)}\big](\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t})=\sum_{i=1}^{M}\frac{\gamma_{m}}{\gamma_{p_{i}}}\bm{\mathbcal{V}^{t}}_{D_{i}}\big[\mathrm{J}\big](\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t}),\;(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t})\in D_{i}\times(0,\mathrm{T}).

(2.75)

Then, we have

\displaystyle u^{(i)}(x,t)

\displaystyle+\vartheta_{i}\ \bm{\mathbcal{V}^{(ii)}}\big[\partial_{t}u^{(i)}\big](\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t})+\alpha_{i}\ \bm{\mathcal{S}^{(ii)}}\big[\partial_{\nu}u^{(i)}\big](\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t})+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{M}\vartheta_{j}\ \bm{\mathbcal{V}^{(ij)}}\big[\partial_{t}u^{(j)}\big](\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t})

\displaystyle+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{M}\alpha_{j}\ \bm{\mathcal{S}^{(ij)}}\big[\partial_{\nu}u^{(j)}\big](\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t})=\frac{\gamma_{m}}{\gamma_{p_{i}}}\bm{\mathbcal{V}^{t}}_{D_{i}}\big[\mathrm{J}\big](\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t})+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{M}\frac{\gamma_{m}}{\gamma_{p_{j}}}\bm{\mathbcal{V}^{(ij)}}\big[\mathrm{J}\big](\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t}),\;(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t})\in D_{i}\times(0,\mathrm{T}).

(2.76)

Then, using the jump relation we arrive at the following boundary integral representation

\displaystyle(1+\frac{\alpha_{i}}{2})\partial_{\nu}u^{(i)}(x,t)+\vartheta_{i}\ \partial_{\nu}\bm{\mathbcal{V}^{(ii)}}\big[\partial_{t}u^{(i)}\big](\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t})+\alpha_{i}\ \bm{\mathcal{K}^{(ii)}}\big[\partial_{\nu}u^{(i)}\big](\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t})+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{M}\vartheta_{j}\ \partial_{\nu}\bm{\mathbcal{V}^{(ij)}}\big[\partial_{t}u^{(j)}\big](\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t})

\displaystyle+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{M}\alpha_{j}\ \partial_{\nu}\bm{\mathcal{S}^{(ij)}}\big[\partial_{\nu}u^{(j)}\big](\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t})=\frac{\gamma_{m}}{\gamma_{p_{i}}}\ \partial_{\nu}\bm{\mathbcal{V}^{(ii)}}\big[\mathrm{J}\big](\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t})+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{M}\frac{\gamma_{m}}{\gamma_{p_{j}}}\ \partial_{\nu}\bm{\mathbcal{V}^{(ij)}}\big[\mathrm{J}\big](\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t}),\;(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t})\in\partial D_{i}\times(0,\mathrm{T}).

(2.77)

Let us now take integration with respect to
\partial D_{i}
to obtain

\displaystyle(1+\frac{\alpha_{i}}{2})\int_{\partial D_{i}}\partial_{\nu}u^{(i)}(x,t)+\underbrace{\vartheta_{i}\int_{\partial D_{i}}\partial_{\nu}\bm{\mathbcal{V}^{(ii)}}_{D}\big[\partial_{t}u^{(i)}\big](\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t})}_{:=\text{err}_{n}^{(1)}}+\underbrace{\alpha_{i}\int_{\partial D_{i}}\bm{\mathcal{K}^{(ii)}}_{\partial D}\big[\partial_{\nu}u^{(i)}\big](\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t})}_{:=\text{err}_{n}^{(2)}}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{M}\vartheta_{j}\int_{\partial D_{i}}\partial_{\nu}\bm{\mathbcal{V}^{(ij)}}\big[\partial_{t}u^{(j)}\big](\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t})}_{:=\text{err}_{n}^{(3)}}+\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{M}\alpha_{j}\int_{\partial D_{i}}\partial_{\nu}\bm{\mathcal{S}^{(ij)}}\big[\partial_{\nu}u^{(j)}\big](\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t})}_{:=\textbf{Term}_{n}^{(1)}}=\underbrace{\int_{\partial D_{i}}\frac{\gamma_{m}}{\gamma_{p_{i}}}\partial_{\nu}\bm{\mathbcal{V}^{(ii)}}\big[\mathrm{J}\big](\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t})}_{:=\textbf{Term}_{n}^{(2)}}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{M}\int_{\partial D_{i}}\frac{\gamma_{m}}{\gamma_{p_{j}}}\partial_{\nu}\bm{\mathbcal{V}^{(ij)}}\big[\mathrm{J}\big](\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t})}_{:=\textbf{err}_{n}^{(4)}}.

(2.78)

Proceeding similarly as we derived the estimates (
2.58
), we can show that

\displaystyle\text{err}_{n}^{(1)}\lesssim\delta^{6-h}.

(2.79)

Again, as discussed in the previous section and due to the estimate (
2.55
), we have

\displaystyle\text{err}_{n}^{(2)}=-\frac{\alpha_{i}}{2}\int_{\partial D_{i}}\partial_{\nu}u^{(i)}+\mathcal{O}(\delta^{6-h}).

(2.80)

We start with the following singularity properties related to the heat fundamental solution.

Lemma 2.4
.

[
33
, Lemma 4.2]

For
x\neq y
and
|x-y|\to 0,
we have

\Big(\int_{0}^{t}|\partial_{\nu_{x}}\Phi(x,t;y,\tau)|^{2}d\tau\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}=\mathcal{O}(|x-y|^{-3}),\;\text{and}\;\Big(\int_{0}^{t}|\nabla_{x}\Phi(x,t;y,\tau)|^{2}d\tau\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}=\mathcal{O}(|x-y|^{-3}),\;t\in(0,T).

(2.81)

Let us now proceed to estimate the term
\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{M}\vartheta_{j}\int_{\partial D_{i}}\partial_{\nu}\bm{\mathbcal{V}^{(ij)}}\big[\partial_{t}u^{(j)}\big](\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t})
. In order to do that, we use divergence theorem and fact that Newtonian potential satisfies the corresponding heat operator to obtain

\displaystyle\text{err}_{n}^{(3)}

\displaystyle:=\Bigg|\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{M}\vartheta_{j}\int_{\partial D_{i}}\partial_{\nu}\bm{\mathbcal{V}^{(ij)}}\big[\partial_{t}u^{(j)}\big](\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t})d\sigma_{x}\Bigg|

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{M}\vartheta_{j}\|1\|_{L^{2}(\partial D_{i})}\|\partial_{\nu}\bm{\mathbcal{V}^{(ij)}}\big[\partial_{t}u^{(j)}\big]\|_{L^{2}(\partial D_{i})}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{M}\delta\vartheta_{j}\Bigg(\int_{\partial D_{i}}\Big(\int_{0}^{t}\int_{D_{j}}\partial_{\nu_{x}}\Phi(x,t;y,\tau)\ \partial_{t}u^{(j)}(\mathrm{y},\tau)dyd\tau\Big)^{2}d\sigma_{x}\Bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\Bigg|\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{M}\frac{\vartheta_{j}}{\kappa_{m}}\delta\int_{0}^{T}\|\partial_{t}u^{(j)}(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{2}(D_{j})}\Bigg(\int_{\partial D_{i}}\int_{D_{j}}\Big(\int_{0}^{t}|\partial_{\nu_{x}}\Phi(x,t;y,\tau)|^{2}d\tau\Big)d\sigma_{x}\Bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\delta\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{M}\frac{\vartheta_{j}}{\kappa_{m}}\|\partial_{t}u^{(j)}(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{2}(D_{j})}|\partial D_{i}\times D_{j}|^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\int_{0}^{t}|\partial_{\nu_{x}}\Phi(x,t;y,\tau)|^{2}d\tau\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\delta^{4+\beta-h}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{M}d_{ij}^{-3}.

(2.82)

Next, we use divergence theorem and the fact that the single layer potential
\mathcal{S}w
satisfies the homogeneous heat equation. That is,
[\alpha\partial_{t}-\Delta_{x}](\mathcal{S}w)(x,t)=0\ \forall(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\partial D\times(0,T)
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\textbf{Term}_{n}^{(1)}:=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{M}\alpha_{j}\int_{\partial D_{i}}\partial_{\nu}\bm{\mathcal{S}^{(ij)}}\big[\partial_{\nu}u^{(j)}\big](\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t})\ dx

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{M}\alpha_{j}\int_{D_{i}}\Delta\bm{\mathcal{S}^{(ij)}}\big[\partial_{\nu}u^{(j)}\big](\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t})\ dx

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{M}\alpha_{j}\int_{D_{i}}\bm{\mathcal{S}^{(ij)}}\big[\partial_{t}\partial_{\nu}u^{(j)}\big](\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t})\ dx.

(2.83)

Let us now define with
\displaystyle\sigma^{(j)}=\int_{\partial D_{j}}\partial_{\nu}u^{(j)}
the following

\displaystyle\bm{\mathcal{S}^{(ij)}}\big[\partial_{t}\sigma^{(j)}\big](\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t})=\frac{1}{\kappa_{m}}\int_{0}^{t}\Phi(z_{i},t;z_{j},\tau)\ \partial_{t}\sigma^{(j)}(\tau)d\tau+\mathcal{A}^{(ij)}+\mathcal{B}^{(ij)}\;\text{with}\ (x,t)\in\partial D_{i}\times(0,T),

(2.84)

where,

\bm{\mathcal{A}^{(ij)}}\big[\sigma^{(j)}\big](\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t}):=\frac{1}{\kappa_{m}}\int_{0}^{t}\big(\Phi(x,t;z_{j},\tau)-\Phi(z_{i},t;z_{j},\tau)\big)\ \partial_{t}\sigma^{(j)}(\tau)d\tau

and

\bm{\mathcal{B}^{(ij)}}\big[\sigma^{(j)}\big](\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t}):=\frac{1}{\kappa_{m}}\int_{0}^{t}\big(\Phi(x,t;y,\tau)-\Phi(x,t;z_{j},\tau)\big)\ \partial_{t}\sigma^{(j)}(\tau)d\tau.

We now proceed to estimate term
\bm{\mathcal{A}^{(ij)}}\big[\sigma^{(j)}\big](\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t}).
We write

\displaystyle\bm{\mathcal{A}^{(ij)}}\big[\sigma^{(j)}\big](\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t})

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\int_{0}^{t}\big(x-z_{i}\big)\cdot\nabla_{x}\Phi(z_{i},t;z_{j},\tau)\ \partial_{t}\sigma^{(j)}(\tau)d\tau}_{:=\bm{\mathcal{A}^{(ij)}}_{1}}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\frac{1}{2}\sum\limits_{k,l=1}^{3}\int_{0}^{t}\big(x_{k}-z_{ik}\big)\big(x_{l}-z_{il}\big)\cdot\partial_{x_{k}}\partial_{x_{l}}\Phi(z^{*}_{i},t;z_{j},\tau)\ \partial_{t}\sigma^{(j)}(\tau)d\tau}_{:=\bm{\mathcal{A}^{(ij)}}_{2}},

where
z^{*}_{i}=z_{i}+\theta(x-z_{i}),0<\theta<1.

Therefore, plugging the previous expression (
2.84
) in (
2.83
), we derive that

\displaystyle\textbf{Term}_{n}^{(1)}:=

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{M}\alpha_{j}\int_{\partial D_{i}}\partial_{\nu}\bm{\mathcal{S}^{(ij)}}\big[\partial_{\nu}u^{(j)}\big](\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t})

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{M}\frac{\alpha_{j}}{\kappa_{m}}\text{vol}(D_{i})\int_{0}^{t}\Phi(z_{i},t;z_{j},\tau)\ \partial_{t}\sigma^{(j)}(\tau)d\tau+\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{M}\frac{\alpha_{j}}{\kappa_{m}}\int_{D_{i}}\mathcal{A}_{1}^{(ij)}}_{:=\textbf{err}_{n}^{(5)}}+\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{M}\frac{\alpha_{j}}{\kappa_{m}}\int_{D_{i}}\mathcal{A}_{2}^{(ij)}}_{:=\textbf{err}_{n}^{(6)}}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{M}\frac{\alpha_{j}}{\kappa_{m}}\int_{D_{i}}\mathcal{B}^{(ij)}}_{:=\textbf{err}_{n}^{(7)}}.

(2.85)

Keeping in mind the definition of
\sigma^{(j)}
, we start with estimating the term
\textbf{err}_{n}^{(4)}
.

\displaystyle\textbf{err}_{n}^{(5)}

\displaystyle:=\Big|\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{M}\frac{\alpha_{j}}{\kappa_{m}}\int_{D_{i}}\mathcal{A}_{1}^{(ij)}\Big|

\displaystyle\lesssim|D_{i}|^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\partial_{t}\partial_{\nu}u^{(j)}(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{2}(\partial D_{j})}\Bigg(\int_{D_{i}}\int_{\partial D_{j}}\Big(\int_{0}^{t}|x-z_{i}|^{2}|\nabla_{x}\Phi(z_{i},t;z_{j},\tau)|^{2}d\tau\Big)d\sigma_{x}d\sigma_{y}\Bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{M}\frac{\alpha_{j}}{\kappa_{m}}|D_{i}|^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\partial_{t}\partial_{\nu}u^{(j)}(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{2}(\partial D_{j})}\ \delta\ |D_{i}\times\partial D_{j}|^{\frac{1}{2}}\ d_{ij}^{-3}

Consequently, we obtain that

\displaystyle\textbf{err}_{n}^{(5)}\lesssim\delta^{6-h}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{M}d_{ij}^{-3}.

(2.86)

In a similar way, we can show that

\displaystyle\textbf{err}_{n}^{(6)}\lesssim\delta^{8-h}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{M}d_{ij}^{-4}\;\text{and}\;\textbf{err}_{n}^{(7)}\lesssim\delta^{6-h}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{M}d_{ij}^{-3}.

(2.87)

Therefore, with these estimates (
2.86
), (
2.87
), and plugging these into (
2.85
), we deduce that

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{M}\frac{\alpha_{j}}{\kappa_{m}}\int_{\partial D_{i}}\partial_{\nu}\bm{\mathcal{S}^{(ij)}}\big[\partial_{\nu}u^{(j)}\big](\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t})=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{M}\frac{\alpha_{j}}{\kappa_{m}}\text{vol}(D_{i})\int_{0}^{t}\Phi(z_{i},t;z_{j},\tau)\ \partial_{t}\sigma^{(j)}(\tau)d\tau+\mathcal{O}\Big(\delta^{6-h}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{M}d_{ij}^{-3}\Big).

(2.88)

Following a similar derivation for the first term on the right-hand side of the expression in (
2.85
) and using the estimate in (
2.54
), we deduce that

\displaystyle\textbf{Term}_{n}^{(2)}

\displaystyle:=\int_{\partial D_{i}}\frac{\gamma_{m}}{\gamma_{p_{i}}}\partial_{\nu}\bm{\mathbcal{V}^{(ii)}}\big[\mathrm{J}\big](\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t})

\displaystyle=\frac{\gamma_{m}}{\gamma_{p_{i}}}\frac{1}{\kappa_{m}}\frac{k\cdot\Im(\varepsilon_{p})}{2\pi}\ f(t)\int_{D_{i}}|\mathrm{E}|^{2}\ d\mathrm{y}+\mathcal{O}(\delta^{\frac{13}{2}-h}).

(2.89)

Next, we turn our attention to estimate the following term

\displaystyle\textbf{err}_{n}^{(4)}

\displaystyle:=\Bigg|\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{M}\int_{\partial D_{i}}\frac{\gamma_{m}}{\gamma_{p_{j}}}\partial_{\nu}\bm{\mathbcal{V}^{(ij)}}\big[\mathrm{J}\big](\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t})d\sigma_{x}\Bigg|

\displaystyle=\Bigg|\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{M}\frac{\gamma_{m}}{\gamma_{p_{j}}}\int_{\partial D_{i}}\Big(\int_{D_{j}}\partial_{\nu_{x}}\Phi(x,t;y,\tau)\ J(\mathrm{y},\tau)dyd\tau\Big)d\sigma_{x}\Bigg|

\displaystyle\lesssim\Bigg|\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{M}\frac{\gamma_{m}}{\gamma_{p_{j}}}|\partial D_{i}|^{\frac{1}{2}}\|f\|_{L^{\infty}}\|J\|_{L^{2}(D_{j})}\Bigg(\int_{\partial D_{i}}\int_{D_{j}}\Big(\int_{0}^{t}|\partial_{\nu_{x}}\Phi(x,t;y,\tau)|^{2}d\tau\Big)\ dyd\sigma_{x}\Bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{M}\frac{\gamma_{m}}{\gamma_{p_{j}}}|\partial D_{i}|^{\frac{1}{2}}\|f\|_{L^{\infty}}\|J\|_{L^{2}(D_{j})}|\partial D_{i}\times D_{j}|^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\int_{0}^{t}|\partial_{\nu_{x}}\Phi(x,t;y,\tau)|^{2}d\tau\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\delta^{5+\beta-h}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{M}d_{ij}^{-3}.

(2.90)

Finally, combining the estimates (
2.79
), (
2.79
), (
2.79
), (
2.88
), (
2.89
), (
2.90
), and plugging those into the expression we obtain that
\displaystyle\sigma^{(i)}=\int_{\partial D_{i}}\partial_{\nu}u^{(i)}
for
i=1,2,\ldots,M
satisfies the system of equations

\displaystyle\sigma^{(i)}+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{M}\frac{\alpha_{j}}{\kappa_{m}}\text{vol}(D_{i})\int_{0}^{t}\Phi(z_{i},t;z_{j},\tau)\ \frac{\partial}{\partial\tau}\sigma^{(j)}(\tau)d\tau=\frac{\gamma_{m}}{\gamma_{p_{i}}}\frac{1}{\kappa_{m}}\frac{k\cdot\Im(\varepsilon_{p})}{2\pi}\ f(t)\int_{D_{i}}|\mathrm{E}|^{2}\ d\mathrm{y}+\mathcal{O}\Big(\delta^{4+\beta-h}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{M}d_{ij}^{-3}\Big).

(2.91)

In the following section, we prove the unique solvability of the above algebraic system and establish an estimate for
\sum\limits_{i=1}^{M}\|\sigma^{(i)}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T)}
.

2.4.1
Unique Solvability of the Discrete Algebraic System in
H^{1}(0,T)

Let us consider the algebraic system (
2.91
)

\displaystyle\sigma^{(i)}+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{M}b_{j}\int_{0}^{t}\Phi(z_{i},t;z_{j},\tau)\frac{\partial}{\partial\tau}\sigma^{(j)}(\tau)\ d\tau=\mathcal{F}_{i}(t).

(2.92)

Since
\sigma^{(i)}(\cdot,0)=0
and using the smoothness of the fundamental solution
\Phi(z_{i},t;z_{j},\tau)
, we apply integration by parts to rewrite the system as

\displaystyle\sigma^{(i)}+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{M}b_{j}\int_{0}^{t}\partial_{t}\Phi(z_{i},t;z_{j},\tau)\sigma^{(j)}(\tau)\ d\tau=\mathcal{F}_{i}(t).

(2.93)

which we rewrite in operator form as

\displaystyle\mathbcal{A}\bm{\sigma}(x,t)=\mathbcal{F}(x,t),

(2.94)

where we aim to invert the operator
\mathbcal{A}:=\bm{\mathrm{I}}+b_{j}\mathbcal{K}
in
L^{2}(0,T)
. Here,

\bm{\sigma}:=\big(\sigma^{(1)},\sigma^{(2)},\ldots,\sigma^{(M)}\big)^{t}
,
\mathbcal{F}:=\big(f_{1},f_{2},\ldots,f_{M}\big)^{t}
and the operator
\mathbcal{K}
is defined as

\mathbcal{K}\bm{\sigma}(t):=\int_{0}^{t}\begin{pmatrix}0&\partial_{t}\Phi(z_{1},t;z_{2},\tau)&\dots&\partial_{t}\Phi(z_{1},t;z_{M},\tau)\\
\partial_{t}\Phi(z_{2},t;z_{1},\tau)&0&\dots&\partial_{t}\Phi(z_{2},t;z_{M},\tau)\\
\vdots&\ddots&\dots&\vdots\\
\partial_{t}\Phi(z_{M},t;z_{1},\tau)&\partial_{t}\Phi(z_{M},t;z_{2},\tau)&\dots&0\end{pmatrix}\bm{\sigma}(\tau)\,d\tau.

(2.95)

It is observed that the fundamental solution
\Phi(z_{i},t;z_{j},\tau)
is smooth for
z_{i}\neq z_{j}
, where
i\neq j
and
i,j=1,2,\ldots,M
. Thus, the matrix-valued kernel

\mathbb{K}(t-\tau;z_{i}-z_{j}):=\begin{pmatrix}0&\partial_{t}\Phi(z_{1},t;z_{2},\tau)&\dots&\partial_{t}\Phi(z_{1},t;z_{M},\tau)\\
\partial_{t}\Phi(z_{2},t;z_{1},\tau)&0&\dots&\partial_{t}\Phi(z_{2},t;z_{M},\tau)\\
\vdots&\ddots&\dots&\vdots\\
\partial_{t}\Phi(z_{M},t;z_{1},\tau)&\partial_{t}\Phi(z_{M},t;z_{2},\tau)&\dots&0\end{pmatrix}

belongs to the
L^{2}(0,T)
function space, implying that
\mathbcal{K}
is a Hilbert-Schmidt operator, and therefore compact in
L^{2}(0,T)
. Compact operators have a discrete spectrum, which implies that the spectrum of
\bm{I}+b_{j}\mathbcal{K}
does not include zero for sufficiently small
b_{j}
, ensuring the invertibility of the operator.
Thus, for small
b_{j}
, the operator
\bm{I}+b_{j}\ \mathbcal{K}
is indeed invertible. However, such a property is not enough. We need to estimate
\Big(\sigma^{(i)}\Big)_{i=1}^{M}
in terms of
\mathbcal{F}
and
\delta.
For this, we state the following lemma.

Lemma 2.5
.

Under the condition

\displaystyle b\ \max\limits_{1\leq i\leq M}\sum\limits_{j\neq i}d_{ij}^{-2}<1,

(2.96)

where
b=\max\limits_{1\leq j\leq M}b_{j}
, with
b_{j}:=\frac{\overline{\alpha}_{j}}{\kappa_{m}}\text{vol}(B_{j})\delta^{3-\beta}
, the algebraic system (
2.91
) is uniquely solvable in
H^{1}(0,T)
. Moreover, the following estimate holds

\displaystyle\Big(\sum\limits_{i=1}^{M}\|\sigma^{(i)}\|^{2}_{H^{1}(0,T)}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq\Big(1-b\ \max\limits_{1\leq i\leq M}\sum\limits_{j\neq i}d_{ij}^{-2}\Big)^{-1}\Big(\sum\limits_{i=1}^{M}\|f_{i}(t)\|^{2}_{H^{1}(0,T)}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}},

(2.97)

where
\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{i}(t):=\frac{\gamma_{m}}{\gamma_{p_{i}}}\frac{1}{\kappa_{m}}\frac{k\cdot\Im(\varepsilon_{p})}{2\pi}f(t)\int_{D_{i}}|\mathrm{E}|^{2}\ d\mathrm{y}
, a function that is constant with respect to the spatial variable
x
.

Proof.

Consider the algebraic system (
2.91
)

\displaystyle\sigma^{(i)}+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{M}b_{j}\int_{0}^{t}\Phi(z_{i},t;z_{j},\tau)\frac{\partial}{\partial\tau}\sigma^{(j)}(\tau)\ d\tau=\mathcal{F}_{i}(t).

(2.98)

We observe that, since
\sigma^{(i)}(0)=0
and
f\in C^{k-1}(\mathbb{R})
, we transform the integral equation as follows

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\Big(\partial_{\tau}\sigma^{(i)}(\tau)+\sum\limits_{j\neq i}b_{j}\Phi(z_{i},t;z_{j},\tau)\partial_{\tau}\sigma^{(j)}(\tau)\Big)\,d\tau=\int_{0}^{t}\partial_{\tau}\mathcal{F}_{i}(\tau)\,d\tau,

(2.99)

which further implies with the assumptions that
\partial_{t}\sigma^{(i)}(t)+\sum\limits_{j\neq i}b_{j}\Phi(z_{i},t;z_{j},\tau)\partial_{t}\sigma^{(j)}(t)-\partial_{t}\mathcal{F}_{i}(t)\geq 0

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\sigma^{(i)}(t)+\sum\limits_{j\neq i}b_{j}\Phi(z_{i},t;z_{j},\tau)\partial_{t}\sigma^{(j)}(t)=\partial_{t}\mathcal{F}_{i}(t),\;\text{a.e for}\ t\in(0,T).

We now set
q_{i}(t):=\partial_{t}\sigma^{(i)}(t)
and
\mathcal{F}(t):=\partial_{t}\mathcal{F}_{i}(t)
Therefore, we rewrite the above equations as follows

\displaystyle q_{i}(t)+\sum\limits_{j\neq i}b_{j}\Phi(z_{i},t;z_{j},\tau)q_{j}(t)=\mathcal{F}_{i}(t).

(2.100)

Multiplying both sides by
q_{i}
, integrating over
[0,T]
, and summing over
i=1,\dots,M
, we obtain

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{i=1}^{M}\int_{0}^{T}q^{2}_{i}(t)dt+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{M}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{M}b_{j}\int_{0}^{T}\Phi(z_{i},t;z_{j},\tau)\ q_{j}(t)\ q_{i}(t)dt=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{M}\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{F}_{i}(t)\ q_{i}(t)\ dt.

(2.101)

We now, observe that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}|\Phi(z_{i},t;z_{j},\tau)\ q_{j}(t)\ q_{i}(t)|dt\leq\Big(\int_{0}^{T}|\Phi(z_{i},t;z_{j},\tau)|^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\int_{0}^{T}|q_{j}(t)\ q_{i}(t)|^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}

(2.102)

Now, consider the following proposition.

Proposition 2.3
.

For
x\neq y
and
|x-y|\to 0
, we have

\displaystyle\Big(\int_{0}^{T}|\Phi(x,t;y,\tau)|^{2}\ dt\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}=\mathcal{O}(|x-y|^{-2}),\quad t\in(0,T).

Then, based on the above Proposition and from (
2.101
), we deduce

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{i=1}^{M}\|q_{i}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T)}-b\sum\limits_{i=1}^{M}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{M}d_{ij}^{-2}\|q_{i}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T)}\leq\Big(\sum\limits_{i=1}^{M}\|q_{i}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T)}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\sum\limits_{i=1}^{M}\|\mathcal{F}_{i}(t)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T)}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}},

which further implies that

\displaystyle\Big(1-b\ \max\limits_{1\leq i\leq M}\sum\limits_{j\neq i}d_{ij}^{-2}\Big)\sum\limits_{i=1}^{M}\|q_{i}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T)}\leq\Big(\sum\limits_{i=1}^{M}\|q_{i}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T)}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\sum\limits_{i=1}^{M}\|\mathcal{F}_{i}(t)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T)}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(2.103)

Consequently, we deduce that

\displaystyle\Big(1-b\ \max\limits_{1\leq i\leq M}\sum\limits_{j\neq i}d_{ij}^{-2}\Big)\Big(\sum\limits_{i=1}^{M}\|q_{i}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T)}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq\Big(\sum\limits_{i=1}^{M}\|\mathcal{F}_{i}(t)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T)}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(2.104)

Thus, based on the above estimate, the unique solvability of (
2.92
) and the estimate (
2.97
) follow from condition (
2.96
) and the observation that
q_{i}(t):=\partial_{t}\sigma^{(i)}(t)
.

The proof is complete.
∎

2.4.2
End of the proof of Theorem
1.1
: The Asymptotic Expansions

In the following, we derive the asymptotic formula for the solution to (
1.4
) in the case of multiple inclusions. The proof is similar to the case of single inclusion, so we skip the details but outline it. we derive for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus D,\ z_{i}\in D_{i}
and
t\in(0,T)
that

\displaystyle u(x,t)=-\sum\limits_{i=1}^{M}\alpha_{i}\frac{1}{\kappa_{m}}\int_{0}^{t}\Phi^{(m)}(x,t;z_{i},\tau)\sigma^{(i)}(\tau)d\tau+\text{error},

(2.105)

where, we set
\displaystyle\sigma^{(i)}:=\Big(\int_{\partial D}\partial_{\nu}u^{(i)}(y,\tau)d\sigma_{y}\Big).
The "error" consists of the following terms and can be estimated with a similar procedure as (
2.61
), (
2.62
), and (
2.60
) as follows:

\displaystyle\text{err}^{(1)}

\displaystyle:=\Big|\sum\limits_{i=1}^{M}\alpha_{i}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\partial D}\Big(\Phi^{(m)}(x,t;y,\tau)-\Phi^{(m)}(x,t;z_{i},\tau)\Big)\partial_{\nu}u(y,\tau)\ d\sigma_{y}d\tau\Big|\lesssim M\delta^{4-h},

\displaystyle\text{err}^{(2)}

\displaystyle:=\Big|\sum\limits_{i=1}^{M}\vartheta_{i}\ \bm{\mathbcal{V}^{t}}_{D}\Big[\partial_{t}u\Big](x,t)\Big|\lesssim M\delta^{2+\beta-h},\ \text{and}

\displaystyle\text{err}^{(3)}

\displaystyle:=\Big|\sum\limits_{i=1}^{M}\frac{1}{\gamma_{p_{i}}}\bm{\mathbcal{V}^{t}}_{D}\Big[\mathrm{J}\Big](x,t)\Big|\lesssim M\delta^{3+\beta-h}.

Therefore, combining above three estimates, we derive that

\displaystyle u(x,t)=-\sum\limits_{i=1}^{M}\alpha_{i}\frac{1}{\kappa_{m}}\int_{0}^{t}\Phi^{(m)}(x,t;z_{i},\tau)\sigma_{i}(\tau)d\tau+\mathcal{O}\big(M\delta^{4-h}\big),

(2.106)

where
\sigma^{(i)}
satisfies the following linear system of equations

\displaystyle\sigma^{(i)}+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{M}\frac{\alpha_{j}}{\kappa_{m}}\text{vol}(D_{i})\int_{0}^{t}\Phi(z_{i},t;z_{j},\tau)\ \frac{\partial}{\partial\tau}\sigma^{(j)}(\tau)d\tau=\frac{\gamma_{m}}{\gamma_{p_{i}}}\frac{1}{\kappa_{m}}\frac{k\cdot\Im(\varepsilon_{p})}{2\pi}\ f(t)\int_{D_{i}}|\mathrm{E}|^{2}\ d\mathrm{y}+\mathcal{O}\Big(\delta^{4+\beta-h}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{M}d_{ij}^{-3}\Big).

This completes the proof. ∎

3
Proof of Theorem
1.2

We begin by considering the following non-homogeneous first-order matrix differential equation, along with its initial conditions, while neglecting error terms of higher order:

\displaystyle\sigma^{(i)}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{M}\frac{\alpha_{j}}{\kappa_{m}}\text{vol}(D_{i})\int_{0}^{t}\Phi^{(m)}(z_{i},t;z_{j},\tau)\frac{\partial}{\partial\tau}\sigma^{(j)}(\tau)\,d\tau=g_{i}(t),\quad\text{for}\;t\in(0,T),

(3.1)

where
\alpha_{i}:=\gamma_{p_{i}}-\gamma_{m}
denotes the contrast between the inner and outer heat coefficients, and
\kappa_{m}:=\frac{c_{m}}{\gamma_{m}}
is the diffusion constant for the background medium. We define
\displaystyle g_{i}(t)=\frac{\gamma_{m}}{\gamma_{p_{i}}}\frac{1}{\kappa_{m}}\frac{k\cdot\Im(\varepsilon_{p})}{2\pi}f(t)\int_{D_{i}}|\mathrm{E}_{i}|^{2}\,d\mathrm{y}.

Based on the a priori estimates for
E_{i}
from Proposition
2.2
, along with the properties described in equations (
1.15
) and (
1.16
), the expression for
g_{i}(t)
can be rewritten in a more tractable form as follows

\int_{D_{i}}|\mathrm{E}_{i}|^{2}=\int_{D_{i}}|\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}(\mathrm{E}_{i})|^{2}+\mathcal{O}(\delta^{3})=E_{i}(z_{i})\cdot\left(\sum_{m=1}^{m_{n_{0}}}\int_{D_{i}}e^{(3)}_{m,n_{0}}\otimes\int_{D_{i}}e^{(3)}_{m,n_{0}}\right)\cdot E_{i}^{\textit{Tr}}(z_{i})+\mathcal{O}(\delta^{3}),

where we define
\mathbb{K}_{D_{i}}
as

\mathbb{K}_{D_{i}}:=\sum_{m=1}^{m_{n_{0}}}\int_{D_{i}}e^{(3)}_{m,n_{0}}\otimes\int_{D_{i}}e^{(3)}_{m,n_{0}}.

Next, recalling the definition
\displaystyle\mathbcal{Q}_{i}=\int_{D_{i}}\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}(\mathrm{E}_{i})
, and applying the spectral decomposition, we obtain the relation

\int_{D_{i}}\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}(\mathrm{E}_{i})=E_{i}(z_{i})\cdot\left(\sum_{m=1}^{m_{n_{0}}}\int_{D_{i}}e^{(3)}_{m,n_{0}}\otimes\int_{D_{i}}e^{(3)}_{m,n_{0}}\right)+\mathcal{O}(\delta^{3}),

which leads to the following expression for
E_{i}(z_{i})

E_{i}(z_{i})=\mathbb{K}_{D_{i}}^{-1}\cdot\int_{D_{i}}\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}(\mathrm{E}_{i}).

Substituting this into the previous equation and neglecting higher-order terms, we derive

\int_{D_{i}}|\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}(\mathrm{E}_{i})|^{2}=\mathbb{K}_{D_{i}}^{-1}\mathbcal{Q}_{i}\cdot\overline{\mathbcal{Q}_{i}^{\textit{Tr}}}.

After normalization, let
e^{(3)}_{m,n_{0}}(B):=\frac{\tilde{e}^{(3)}_{m,n_{0}}}{\|\tilde{e}^{(3)}_{m,n_{0}}\|_{L^{2}(B)}}
, where
\|\tilde{e}^{(3)}_{m,n_{0}}\|_{L^{2}(B)}=\mathcal{O}(\delta^{-\frac{3}{2}})
represents the scaled eigenfunctions associated with the space
\nabla\mathbb{H}_{\textit{arm}}
on the domain
B
. Using the property (
1.16
) and the definition of the polarization matrix
\mathbcal{P}_{D_{i}}

\mathbcal{P}_{D_{i}}=\frac{\delta^{3}}{1+\eta\lambda_{n_{0}}^{(3)}}\sum_{m=1}^{m_{n_{0}}}\int_{B}e^{(3)}_{m,n_{0}}(B)\otimes\int_{B}e^{(3)}_{m,n_{0}}(B)+\mathcal{O}(\delta^{3}),

we observe that

\mathbb{K}_{D_{i}}=\delta^{h}\,\mathbcal{P}_{D_{i}}.

Thus, from the previous expression, we obtain

\int_{D_{i}}|\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}(\mathrm{E}_{i})|^{2}=\delta^{3-2h}\mathbcal{P}_{B}\ \mathbcal{Q}_{i}\cdot\overline{\mathbcal{Q}_{i}^{\textit{Tr}}},

where
\mathbcal{P}_{B}
is defined as
\displaystyle\mathbcal{P}_{B}:=\sum_{m=1}^{m_{n_{0}}}\int_{B}e^{(3)}_{m,n_{0}}(B)\otimes\int_{B}e^{(3)}_{m,n_{0}}(B).

Thus, we can rewrite the algebraic system in equation (
3.1
) in terms of the scaled variable
\displaystyle\tilde{\sigma}^{(i)}:=\frac{1}{\text{vol}(D_{i})}\int_{\partial D_{i}}\partial_{\nu}u^{(i)}
for
i=1,2,\ldots,M
:

\displaystyle\tilde{\sigma}^{(i)}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{M}\frac{\alpha_{j}}{\kappa_{m}}\text{vol}(D_{j})\int_{0}^{t}\Phi^{(m)}(z_{i},t;z_{j},\tau)\frac{\partial}{\partial\tau}\tilde{\sigma}^{(j)}(\tau)\,d\tau=\overline{a}\,\delta^{\beta-h}\,f(t)\,\mathbcal{P}_{B}\ \mathbcal{Q}_{i}\cdot\overline{\mathbcal{Q}_{i}^{\textit{Tr}}},

(3.2)

where we have defined
\overline{a}:=\frac{\gamma_{m}}{\overline{\gamma}_{p_{i}}}\frac{k\cdot\overline{\varepsilon}_{p}}{2\pi\kappa_{m}}
, which is independent of
\delta
. Since
\mathbcal{P}_{D_{i}}=\delta^{3-h}\mathbcal{P}_{B}
, we rewrite the algebraic system in the scaled domain
B
, where
\tilde{\mathbcal{Q}}_{i}
satisfies the following algebraic system as discussed in Lemma
3.3

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbcal{Q}}_{i}+\eta\sum_{j\neq i}^{M}\bm{\Upsilon}^{(k)}(z_{i},z_{j})\,\mathbcal{P}_{D_{j}}\cdot\tilde{\mathbcal{Q}}_{j}=\mathrm{E}^{\textit{in}}(z_{i}).

(3.3)

We now assume that the shape of the each nanoparticles
D_{j}
for
j=1,2,\ldots,M
is same, which implies that
b_{i}=b_{j}
for
i,j=1,2,\ldots,M.
Let us define
\overline{b}:=\overline{b}_{j}
be the scaled value of
b_{j}
i.e.
\overline{b}=\frac{\overline{\alpha}_{j}}{\kappa_{m}}\text{vol}(B_{j}).

The next critical step involves establishing a comparison between equation (
3.2
) and the following Lippmann-Schwinger equation

\displaystyle\bm{\mathrm{Y}}(x,t)+\overline{b}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbf{\Omega}}\Phi^{(m)}(x,t;y,\tau)\frac{\partial}{\partial\tau}\bm{\mathrm{Y}}(y,\tau)\,dy\,d\tau=\mathbcal{F}(x,t),\quad\text{for}\;(x,t)\in\mathbf{\Omega}\times(0,T),

(3.4)

where we have defined
\displaystyle\mathbcal{F}(x,t):=\overline{a}\,f(t)\,\mathbcal{A}_{B}\cdot\mathbcal{P}_{B}^{-1}\cdot\mathbcal{A}_{B}\cdot\bm{E}_{f}\cdot\overline{\bm{E}}^{\textit{Tr}}_{f}
. The initial condition for
\bm{\mathrm{Y}}
is implicitly zero in this equation. Here,
\tilde{\bm{E}}_{f}
is the solution to the following effective Lippmann-Schwinger equation

\displaystyle\bm{E}_{f}(x)+\Big(\mathbb{M}_{\bm{\Omega}}^{(\kappa)}-k^{2}\mathbb{N}_{\bm{\Omega}}^{(\kappa)}\Big)\big[\mathbcal{A}_{B}\cdot\bm{E}_{f}\big](x)=\mathrm{E}^{\textit{in}}(x),\quad\text{for}\;x\in\bm{\Omega},

(3.5)

where
\mathbcal{A}_{B}
is the effective polarization matrix corresponding to the algebraic system defined in equation (
3.3
).

Let us now define

\displaystyle\mathbcal{V}(x,t):=\begin{cases}\bm{\mathrm{Y}}(x,t)&\text{if}\;(x,t)\in\mathbf{\Omega}\times(0,T)\\
\displaystyle\mathbcal{F}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t})-\overline{b}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbf{\Omega}}\Phi^{(m)}(x,t;y,\tau)\ \frac{\partial}{\partial\tau}\bm{\mathrm{Y}}(y,\tau)\ dyd\tau&\text{for}\;(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\mathbf{\Omega}\times(0,T).\end{cases}

(3.6)

We set
\mathbcal{W}(x,t):=\mathbcal{F}(x,t)-\mathbcal{V}(x,t).
Therefore, we have the following result.

Lemma 3.1
.

If
\bm{\mathrm{Y}}
is the solution of the Lippmann-Schwinger equation (
3.4
), then
\mathbcal{W}
satisfies the following problem

\displaystyle\begin{cases}(\kappa_{m}\partial_{t}-\Delta)\mathbcal{W}+\overline{b}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{\mathbf{\Omega}}\partial_{t}\mathbcal{W}=\overline{b}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{\mathbf{\Omega}}\mathbcal{F}(x,t)&\text{if}\;(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\times(0,T)\\
\mathbcal{W}(x,0)=0&\text{for}\;x\in\mathbb{R}^{3},\\
|\mathbcal{W}(x,t)|\leq C_{0}\ e^{A|x|^{2}}&\text{as}\ |x|\to+\infty,\end{cases}

(3.7)

for some positive constant
C_{0}
and
A\leq\frac{1}{4T}.
The above differential equation has a unique solution in
\mathrm{H}^{r}_{0,\sigma}\big(0,\mathrm{T};\mathrm{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\big)
for
\mathbcal{F}\in\mathrm{H}^{r-\frac{1}{2}}_{0,\sigma}\big(0,\mathrm{T};\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\big)
.

We can show this result in a manner similar to the approach discussed in Section
2.2
. The only difference is the presence of a perturbation in the first-order term, which will not affect the overall proof of well-posedness.

3.1
Solvability and Regularity of the Effective Integral Equation for the Parabolic Problem

Lemma 3.2
.

The Lippmann-Schwinger equation

\bm{\mathrm{Y}}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t})+\overline{b}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbf{\Omega}}\Phi^{(m)}(x,t;y,\tau)\ \frac{\partial}{\partial\tau}\bm{\mathrm{Y}}(y,\tau)\ dyd\tau=\mathbcal{F}(x,t),\;(x,t)\in\mathbf{\Omega}\times(0,\mathrm{T}),

(3.8)

has a unique solution in

\mathrm{H}^{r}_{0,\sigma}\big(0,\mathrm{T};\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbf{\Omega})\big)
for
f\in\mathrm{H}^{r+\frac{1}{2}}_{0,\sigma}\big(0,\mathrm{T};\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbf{\Omega})\big)
and it satisfies

\|\bm{\mathrm{Y}}\|_{\mathrm{H}^{r}_{0,\sigma}\big(0,\mathrm{T};\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbf{\Omega})\big)}\lesssim\|f\|_{\mathrm{H}^{r+\frac{1}{2}}_{0,\sigma}\big(0,\mathrm{T};\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbf{\Omega})\big)}.

Proof.

We begin by considering the Lippmann-Schwinger equation in the Fourier-Laplace domain, where the transform parameter is set as
\bm{s}\in\mathbb{C}_{+}:=\Big\{\bm{s}\in\mathbb{C}:\Re(\bm{s})>0\Big\}
. This choice is motivated by the fact that the heat equation, when transformed into the Laplace domain, assumes the form
\Big(-\Delta+\left(\bm{s}^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)^{2}\Big)\hat{u}=\hat{\mathbcal{F}}(x,\bm{s})
. Consequently, it is necessary to consider
\bm{s}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus(-\infty,0]
, which ensures that
\bm{s}^{\frac{1}{2}}\in\mathbb{C}_{+}
and
\omega=\Re(\bm{s}^{\frac{1}{2}})>0
.

Next, we introduce the Newtonian heat potential operator, which is defined as

\bm{\mathrm{V}}^{t}_{\mathbf{\Omega}}[f](x,t):=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbf{\Omega}}\Phi^{(m)}(x,t;y,\tau)\,f(y,\tau)\,dy\,d\tau.

Here, we note that the Laplace-Fourier transform
\mathrm{G}^{(\bm{s})}
of the fundamental solution
\Phi^{(m)}(x,t;y,\tau)
for the heat equation corresponds to the fundamental solution of the differential equation
-\Delta u+\bm{s}u=0
, expressed as

\mathrm{G}^{(\bm{s})}(x,y)=\frac{1}{4\pi|x-y|}e^{-\sqrt{\bm{s}}\,|x-y|}.

Based on this, the corresponding Newtonian potential is defined as

\bm{\mathrm{V}}_{\bm{s},\mathbf{\Omega}}[\hat{f}](x):=\int_{\Omega}\mathrm{G}^{(\bm{s})}(x,y)\,\hat{f}(y)\,dy,

where
\displaystyle\widehat{u}:=\widehat{u}(x,\mathbf{s})=\int_{0}^{\infty}u(x,t)e^{-\mathbf{s}t}dt.
Furthermore, it is known that for
\hat{h}(y)\in L^{2}(\bm{\Omega})
, extended by 0, the Newtonian potential
z:=\bm{\mathrm{V}}_{\bm{s},\mathbf{\Omega}}[\hat{h}]
satisfies the following equation

-\Delta z+\bm{s}z=\hat{h}\quad\text{in}\ \mathbb{R}^{3}.

(3.9)

Thus, in the Laplace-Fourier domain, the time-domain Lippmann-Schwinger equation (
3.4
) takes the form

\displaystyle\hat{\bm{\mathrm{Y}}}+\overline{b}\ \mathbf{s}\;\bm{\mathrm{V}}_{\bm{s},\mathbf{\Omega}}\big(\hat{\bm{\mathrm{Y}}}\big)=\hat{\mathbcal{F}}(x,\bm{s})\quad\text{in}\;\mathbf{\Omega}.

(3.10)

Our goal is to establish the well-posedness of this problem using the approach outlined in
[
21
]
. Specifically, we will formulate a variational framework for this problem and demonstrate its well-posedness using the Lax-Milgram Lemma.

To proceed, we multiply equation (
3.10
) by
\widehat{g}\in L^{2}(\mathbf{\Omega})
and integrate over
\mathbf{\Omega}
, obtaining the following variational form for
\widehat{\bm{\mathrm{Y}}}\in L^{2}(\mathbf{\Omega})
:

\mathbb{A}\big(\hat{\bm{\mathrm{Y}}},\widehat{g}\big)=\mathbb{B}(\widehat{g})\quad\text{in}\;L^{2}(\mathbf{\Omega}),

where

\mathbb{A}\big(\hat{\bm{\mathrm{Y}}},\widehat{g}\big):=\int_{\mathbf{\Omega}}\hat{\bm{\mathrm{Y}}}+\mathbf{s}\ \overline{b}\ \bm{\mathrm{V}}_{\bm{s},\mathbf{\Omega}}\big(\hat{\bm{\mathrm{Y}}}\big)\Big)\,\overline{\widehat{g}}\,dy,

and

\mathbb{B}(\widehat{g}):=\int_{\mathbf{\Omega}}\widehat{\mathbcal{F}}\,\overline{\widehat{g}}\,dy.

To prove the coercivity of this variational form, we choose
\widehat{g}=\bm{s}^{\frac{1}{2}}\widehat{\bm{\mathrm{Y}}}
, leading to the following:

\mathbb{A}\big(\hat{\bm{\mathrm{Y}}},\bm{s}^{\frac{1}{2}}\widehat{\bm{\mathrm{Y}}}\big)=\overline{\bm{s}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\int_{\mathbf{\Omega}}|\hat{\bm{\mathrm{Y}}}|^{2}\,dy+\mathbf{s}\overline{\bm{s}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\ \overline{b}\int_{\mathbf{\Omega}}\bm{\mathrm{V}}_{\bm{s},\mathbf{\Omega}}\big(\hat{\bm{\mathrm{Y}}}\big)\,\overline{\widehat{\bm{\mathrm{Y}}}}\,dy.

Taking the real part of this equation, we have

\Re\Big(\mathbb{A}\big(\hat{\bm{\mathrm{Y}}},\bm{s}^{\frac{1}{2}}\widehat{\bm{\mathrm{Y}}}\big)\Big)=\omega\int_{\mathbf{\Omega}}|\hat{\bm{\mathrm{Y}}}|^{2}\,dy+\Re\Big(\mathbf{s}\overline{\bm{s}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\ \overline{b}\int_{\mathbf{\Omega}}\bm{\mathrm{V}}_{\bm{s},\mathbf{\Omega}}\big(\hat{\bm{\mathrm{Y}}}\big)\,\overline{\widehat{\bm{\mathrm{Y}}}}\,dy\Big).

From this, using equation (
3.9
), we deduce that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\bm{\mathrm{V}}_{\bm{s},\mathbf{\Omega}}\big(\hat{\bm{\mathrm{Y}}}\big)\,\overline{\widehat{\bm{\mathrm{Y}}}}\,dy=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}z\,\overline{\Big(-\Delta z+\bm{\mathrm{s}}z\Big)}\,dy=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}|\nabla z|^{2}+\overline{\bm{\mathrm{s}}}|z|^{2}\,dy.

Thus, we find that

\Re\Big(\mathbf{s}\overline{\bm{s}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\ \overline{b}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\bm{\mathrm{V}}_{\bm{s},\mathbf{\Omega}}\big(\hat{\bm{\mathrm{Y}}}\big)\,\overline{\widehat{\bm{\mathrm{Y}}}}\,dy\Big)=\Re\Big(\mathbf{s}\overline{\bm{s}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\ \overline{b}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}|\nabla z|^{2}\,dy+|\bm{\mathrm{s}}|^{2}\overline{\bm{s}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\ \overline{b}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}|z|^{2}\,dy\Big)\geq 0.

Thus, we conclude that

\displaystyle\mathbb{A}\big(\hat{\bm{\mathrm{Y}}},\bm{s}^{\frac{1}{2}}\widehat{\bm{\mathrm{Y}}}\big)\geq\omega\|\hat{\bm{\mathrm{Y}}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbf{\Omega})}^{2}.

(3.11)

Moreover, we have

\displaystyle|\mathbb{B}(\widehat{\bm{\mathrm{Y}}})|=\Big|\int_{\mathbf{\Omega}}\widehat{\mathbcal{F}}\,\overline{\mathbf{s}^{\frac{1}{2}}\widehat{\bm{\mathrm{Y}}}}\,dy\Big|=\Big|\overline{\mathbf{s}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\int_{\mathbf{\Omega}}\widehat{\mathbcal{F}}\,\hat{\bm{\mathrm{Y}}}\,dx\Big|\leq|\mathbf{s}|^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\widehat{f}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbf{\Omega})}\|\hat{\bm{\mathrm{Y}}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbf{\Omega})}.

(3.12)

Thus, combining (
3.11
) and (
3.12
), it follows that

\displaystyle\Big\|\Big(\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{s}\bm{\mathrm{V}}_{\bm{s},\mathbf{\Omega}}\Big)^{-1}\Big\|_{L^{2}(\mathbf{\Omega})\to L^{2}(\mathbf{\Omega})}\leq\frac{\bm{s}^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\omega},

(3.13)

where, we observe it satisfies the form (
1.9
) and hence it allows us to apply Lemma
1.1
. Therefore, we define the corresponding time-domain operator

\bm{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathbf{\Omega}}:=\mathbf{I}+\bm{\mathrm{V}}^{t}_{\mathbf{\Omega}}\partial_{t}.

Following the notation of Lubich et al.
[
22
,
23
]
and Lemma
1.1
, and utilizing the Laplace-Fourier techniques presented in
[
24
,
33
,
21
]
, we can show that

\bm{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathbf{\Omega}}^{-1}:H^{r+\frac{1}{2}}_{0,\sigma}(0,T;L^{2}(\mathbf{\Omega}))\to H^{r}_{0,\sigma}(0,T;L^{2}(\mathbf{\Omega}))

is a bounded operator.

Thus, we conclude that equation (
3.4
) has a unique solution in
H^{r}_{0,\sigma}(0,T;L^{2}(\mathbf{\Omega}))
for
f\in H^{r+\frac{1}{2}}_{0,\sigma}(0,T;L^{2}(\mathbf{\Omega}))
, completing the proof.
∎

We now present the following corollary, derived as a direct consequence of the previous lemma. This corollary will play a crucial role in the subsequent sections. Specifically, the corollary asserts the following

Corollary 3.1
.

Consider the Lippmann-Schwinger equation (
3.4
). Then, we have
\partial_{t}^{2}\bm{\mathrm{Y}}\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}\big(0,\mathrm{T};L^{\infty}(\mathbf{\Omega})\big)
and
\partial_{\mathrm{x}_{i}}\partial_{t}\bm{\mathrm{Y}}\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,\mathrm{T};L^{p}(\mathbf{\Omega})\big)
for any
p>3.

Proof.

We begin by recalling the Lippmann-Schwinger equation (
3.4
)

\bm{\mathrm{Y}}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t})+\overline{b}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbf{\Omega}}\Phi^{(m)}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t};\mathrm{y},\tau)\ \frac{\partial}{\partial\tau}\bm{\mathrm{Y}}(\mathrm{y},\tau)\,d\mathrm{y}d\tau=\mathbcal{F}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t}),\quad(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t})\in\mathbf{\Omega}\times(0,\mathrm{T}),

which can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\bm{\mathrm{Y}}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t})+\overline{b}\mathbf{\bm{V}}^{t}_{\bm{\Omega}}\left[\partial_{t}\bm{\mathrm{Y}}\right](\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t})=\mathbcal{F}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t}),\quad(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t})\in\mathbf{\Omega}\times(0,\mathrm{T}).

(3.14)

By applying Lemma
3.2
, we observe that for
\mathbcal{F}\in\mathrm{H}^{\frac{11}{2}}_{0,\sigma}(0,\mathrm{T};\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbf{\Omega}))
, it follows that
\bm{\mathrm{Y}}\in\mathrm{H}^{5}_{0,\sigma}(0,\mathrm{T};\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbf{\Omega}))
(i.e.,
p=5
), which further implies
\partial_{t}\bm{\mathrm{Y}}\in\mathrm{H}^{4}_{0,\sigma}(0,\mathrm{T};\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbf{\Omega}))
.

Next, using Proposition
2.1
, we conclude that
\mathbf{\bm{V}}^{t}_{\bm{\Omega}}\left[\partial_{t}\bm{\mathrm{Y}}\right]\in\mathrm{H}^{\frac{7}{2}}_{0,\sigma}(0,\mathrm{T};\mathrm{H}^{2}(\mathbf{\Omega}))
. By the continuous Sobolev embedding
H^{2}(\mathbf{\Omega})\hookrightarrow L^{\infty}(\mathbf{\Omega})
, this implies
\mathbf{\bm{V}}^{t}_{\bm{\Omega}}\left[\partial_{t}\bm{\mathrm{Y}}\right]\in\mathrm{H}^{3}_{0,\sigma}(0,\mathrm{T};L^{\infty}(\mathbf{\Omega}))
.

Since
\mathbcal{F}\in\mathrm{H}^{3}_{0,\sigma}(0,\mathrm{T};L^{\infty}(\mathbf{\Omega}))
, it follows that
\bm{\mathrm{Y}}\in\mathrm{H}^{\frac{7}{2}}_{0,\sigma}(0,\mathrm{T};L^{\infty}(\mathbf{\Omega}))
. Consequently, we deduce that
\partial_{t}^{2}\bm{\mathrm{Y}}\in\mathrm{H}^{\frac{3}{2}}_{0,\sigma}(0,\mathrm{T};L^{\infty}(\mathbf{\Omega}))
, and by the Sobolev embedding
H^{r}(0,T)\xhookrightarrow{}\mathcal{C}(0,T)
for
r>\frac{1}{2}
, we obtain
\partial_{t}^{2}\bm{\mathrm{Y}}\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,\mathrm{T};L^{\infty}(\mathbf{\Omega}))
.

We now refer to classical singularity estimates of the fundamental solution
\Phi^{(m)}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t};\mathrm{y},\tau)
, as outlined in
[
12
, Chapter 1]
and
[
17
, Chapter 9]
. A critical inequality in this context is
\mathrm{s}^{\mathrm{r}}e^{-\mathrm{s}}\leq\mathrm{r}^{\mathrm{r}}e^{-\mathrm{r}}
, where
0<\mathrm{s},\mathrm{r}<\infty
and
\mathrm{s}=\kappa_{m}|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|^{2}/4(t-\tau)
. Utilizing this, we derive the following singularity estimates

\begin{cases}|\Phi(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t};\mathrm{y},\tau)|\lesssim\frac{\kappa_{m}^{\mathrm{r}}}{(t-\tau)^{\mathrm{r}}}\frac{1}{|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|^{3-2\mathrm{r}}},&\quad\mathrm{r}<\frac{3}{2},\\[10.0pt]
|\partial_{\mathrm{x}_{i}}\Phi(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t};\mathrm{y},\tau)|\lesssim\frac{\kappa_{m}^{\mathrm{r}}}{(t-\tau)^{\mathrm{r}}}\frac{1}{|\mathrm{x}-\mathrm{y}|^{4-2\mathrm{r}}},&\quad\mathrm{r}<\frac{5}{2},\ i=1,2,\end{cases}

(3.15)

for
0\leq\tau\leq t\leq T
and
\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
with
\mathrm{x}\neq\mathrm{y}
.

We first observe that

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{\mathrm{x}_{i}}\partial_{t}\bm{\mathrm{Y}}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t})\right|\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbf{\Omega}}|\partial_{\mathrm{x}_{i}}\Phi^{(m)}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t};\mathrm{y},\tau)|\,d\mathrm{y}d\tau\cdot\big\|\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\tau^{2}}\bm{\mathrm{Y}}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,\mathrm{T};L^{\infty}(\mathbf{\Omega}))}+|\partial_{\mathrm{x}_{i}}\partial_{t}\mathbcal{F}(x,t)|.

(3.16)

Following the analysis in
[
9
, Section 5.1]
, we observe that
\tilde{\bm{E}}_{f}
belongs to
W^{1,p}(\bm{\Omega})
for any
p>3
. Additionally, we consider
f(t)
within the function space
\mathrm{H}^{\frac{5}{2}}_{0,\sigma}(0,\mathrm{T})
, which ensures that
\mathbcal{F}\in\mathrm{H}^{\frac{5}{2}}_{0,\sigma}\big(0,\mathrm{T};W^{1,p}(\bm{\Omega})\big)
for any
p>3
.

Utilizing the singularity estimate for
\partial_{\mathrm{x}_{i}}\Phi^{(m)}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t};\mathrm{y},\tau)
with
r>\frac{1}{2}
, we derive

\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbf{\Omega}}|\partial_{\mathrm{x}_{i}}\Phi^{(m)}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{t};\mathrm{y},\tau)|\,d\mathrm{y}\,d\tau=\mathcal{O}(1),

which implies that
\partial_{\mathrm{x}_{i}}\partial_{t}\bm{\mathrm{Y}}\in\mathrm{H}^{\frac{3}{2}}_{0,\sigma}\big(0,\mathrm{T};L^{p}(\mathbf{\Omega})\big)
for any
p>3
. Specifically, we conclude that
\partial_{\mathrm{x}_{i}}\partial_{t}\bm{\mathrm{Y}}\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}\big(0,\mathrm{T};L^{p}(\mathbf{\Omega})\big)
for any
p>3
.

This completes the proof.
∎

To complete the proof of Theorem
1.2
, we address the effective electric field that arises in the effective parabolic problem (
3.7
). In the subsequent sections, we analyze the associated electromagnetic problem and present the relevant results.

3.2
Proof of Proposition
2.2
: The Effective Electromagnetic Problem

In this section, we present the asymptotic analysis of the solution to the electromagnetic scattering problem (
1.2
) for a cluster of nanoparticles
D_{j}
for
j=1,2,\ldots,M
, in the limit as
\delta\to 0
, where the plasmonic nanoparticles are distributed within the region of interest
\bm{\Omega}
.

We start by introducing the Lippmann-Schwinger (LS) equation, which provides the solution to the electromagnetic scattering problem (
1.2
) for
i=1,2,\ldots,M
. The equation is formulated as

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}_{i}(\mathrm{x})+\eta\sum_{i=1}^{M}\mathbb{M}_{D_{i}}^{(\kappa)}\big[\mathrm{E}_{i}\big](\mathrm{x})-k^{2}\eta\sum_{i=1}^{M}\mathbb{N}_{D_{i}}^{(\kappa)}\big[\mathrm{E}_{i}\big](\mathrm{x})=\mathrm{E}^{\textit{in}}(\mathrm{x}),\quad\text{for}\ x\in D:=\bigcup_{i=1}^{M}D_{i},

(3.17)

where the magnetization operator and the Newtonian operator are given by:

\mathbb{M}_{D_{i}}^{(\kappa)}\big[\mathrm{E}\big](\mathrm{x})=\nabla\int_{D_{i}}\nabla\mathcal{G}^{(\kappa)}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})\cdot\mathrm{E}(\mathrm{y})\,d\mathrm{y},\quad\mathbb{N}_{D_{i}}^{(\kappa)}\big[\mathrm{E}\big](\mathrm{x})=\int_{D_{i}}\mathcal{G}^{(\kappa)}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})\,\mathrm{E}(\mathrm{y})\,d\mathrm{y}.

Here,
\mathcal{G}^{(\kappa)}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})
represents the Green’s function corresponding to the Helmholtz operator, and the contrast coefficient
\eta
is defined as:

\eta:=\varepsilon_{p}-\varepsilon_{m},

(3.18)

where
\varepsilon_{p}
and
\varepsilon_{m}
are the permittivities of the plasmonic material and the surrounding medium, respectively.

Next, we study the LS equation in
\mathbb{L}^{2}(D_{i}):=(L^{2}(D_{i}))^{3}
. To do this, we project the equation (
3.17
), onto the decomposition of
\mathbb{L}^{2}(D_{i})
, into three sub-spaces

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}^{2}(D_{i})=\mathbb{H}_{0}(\textit{div}\ 0,D_{i})\oplus\mathbb{H}_{0}(\textit{curl}\ 0,D_{i})\oplus\nabla\mathbb{H}_{\textit{arm}},

where
\mathbb{H}_{0}(\textit{div}\ 0,D_{i})
and
\mathbb{H}_{0}(\textit{curl}\ 0,D_{i})
are the sub-spaces corresponding to divergence-free and curl-free fields, respectively, and
\nabla\mathbb{H}_{\textit{arm}}
represents the gradient of harmonic functions.

Finally, we are interested in analyzing the behavior of the system in the regime where:

\displaystyle M\sim d^{-3},\quad\text{and}\quad d\sim\delta^{\lambda},\quad\text{for some non-negative parameter}\ \lambda.

(3.19)

This scaling regime characterizes the relationship between the number of nanoparticles
M
, the inter-particle distance
d
, and the small parameter
\delta
.

3.2.1
Construction of the Algebraic System for the Electromagnetic Problem

Lemma 3.3
.

The vectors
\displaystyle\mathbcal{Q}_{i}:=\int_{D_{i}}\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{i})(y)\,dy
for
i=1,2,\ldots,M
satisfy the following linear algebraic system

\displaystyle\mathbcal{Q}_{i}-\eta\sum_{j\neq i}^{M}\mathbcal{P}_{D_{i}}\cdot\bm{\Upsilon}^{(k)}(z_{i},z_{j})\ \mathbcal{Q}_{j}=\mathbcal{P}_{D_{i}}\cdot\mathrm{E}^{\textit{in}}(z_{i})+\mathcal{O}\left(\delta^{\min\{4-h,7-2h-4\lambda\}}\right),\;\text{for}\ j=1,2,\ldots,M,

where
\mathbcal{P}_{D_{i}}
is the polarization matrix, defined as:

\displaystyle\mathbcal{P}_{D_{i}}=\delta^{3}\sum_{n}\frac{1}{1+\eta\lambda_{n}^{(3)}}\left(\int_{B_{i}}e^{(3)}_{n}\otimes\int_{B_{i}}e^{(3)}_{n}\right),

with
e^{(3)}_{n}(\cdot)
denoting the eigenfunctions associated
\nabla\mathbb{H}_{\textit{arm}}
-space in the domain
B_{i}
.

In addition, the algebraic system described above is invertible under the following condition

\displaystyle|\eta|\max_{j=1,2,\ldots,M}\|\mathbcal{P}_{D_{i}}\|\ d^{-3}<1.

Proof.

We begin by recalling the Lippmann-Schwinger equation for the electric field
\mathrm{E}_{i}(\mathrm{x})
within the nanoparticle’s cluster

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}_{i}(\mathrm{x})+\eta\,\mathbb{M}_{D_{i}}^{(k)}\big[\mathrm{E}_{i}\big](\mathrm{x})-k^{2}\eta\,\mathbb{N}_{D_{i}}^{(\kappa)}\big[\mathrm{E}_{i}\big](\mathrm{x})=\mathrm{E}^{\textit{in}}(\mathrm{x})-\eta\sum_{j\neq i}^{M}\left(\mathbb{M}_{D_{j}}^{(\kappa)}-k^{2}\mathbb{N}_{D_{j}}^{(\kappa)}\right)\big[\mathrm{E}_{j}\big](\mathrm{x}),\;x\in D_{i}

(3.20)

where
\eta=\varepsilon_{p}-\varepsilon_{m}
. We express the operators
\mathbb{M}_{D_{j}}^{(\kappa)}
and
\mathbb{N}_{D_{j}}^{(\kappa)}
using the dyadic Green’s function
\bm{\Upsilon}^{(k)}(x,y)
, given by

\displaystyle\bm{\Upsilon}^{(k)}(x,y)=\underset{x}{\text{Hess}}\,\mathcal{G}^{(k)}(x,y)+k^{2}\mathcal{G}^{(k)}(x,y)\mathbb{I},

where
\mathcal{G}^{(k)}(x,y)
is the Green’s function of the Helmholtz equation. This allows us to write

\displaystyle\left(-\mathbb{M}_{D_{j}}^{(\kappa)}+k^{2}\mathbb{N}_{D_{j}}^{(\kappa)}\right)\big[\mathrm{E}_{j}\big](\mathrm{x})=\int_{D_{j}}\bm{\Upsilon}^{(k)}(x,y)\,\mathrm{E}_{j}(y)\,dy,\;\text{for}\ x\notin D_{j}.

Let us define
\mathbb{L}
as

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}:=\left(\mathbb{I}+\eta\,\mathbb{M}_{D_{i}}^{(-k)}-k^{2}\eta\mathbb{N}_{D_{i}}^{(-k)}\right)^{-1}(\mathbb{I}):=\mathbb{T}_{-k}^{-1}(\mathbb{I}),

to simplify the Lippmann-Schwinger equation. Next, we decompose the electric field
\mathrm{E}_{j}
into its projections
\mathrm{E}_{j}=\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{j})+\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{j}),

where
\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{j})
is the component in
\mathbb{H}_{0}(\textit{div}\ 0,D_{j})
, and
\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{j})
is the component in
\nabla\mathbb{H}_{\textit{arm}}
. Observe that
\overset{2}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{j})=0
, as

\int_{D_{j}}E_{j}\cdot e_{n}^{(2)}=\frac{1}{k^{2}}\int_{D_{j}}\delta_{j}^{-1}\,\text{curl}\,\text{curl}\,E_{j}\cdot e_{n}^{(2)}=0,

by integration by parts, using the fact that
\text{curl}\,e_{n}^{(2)}=0
and
e_{n}^{(2)}\times\nu=0
on
\partial D_{j}
.

Integrating both sides of the Lippmann-Schwinger equation (
3.20
) over the domain
D_{i}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{D_{i}}\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{i})(y)\ dy-\eta\sum_{j\neq i}^{M}\int_{D_{i}}\mathbb{L}(x)\cdot

\displaystyle\int_{D_{j}}\bm{\Upsilon}^{(k)}(x,y)\ \overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{j})(y)dydx=\int_{D_{i}}\mathbb{L}(x)\cdot\mathrm{E}^{\textit{in}}(x)dx

\displaystyle+\eta\sum_{j\neq i}^{M}\int_{D_{i}}\mathbb{L}(x)\cdot\int_{D_{j}}\bm{\Upsilon}^{(k)}(x,y)\ \overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{j})(y)dydx

Since
\mathbb{M}_{D_{j}}^{(\kappa)}
vanishes on
\mathbb{H}_{0}(\textit{div}\ 0,D_{j})
, the term involving
\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{j})
simplifies to

\int_{D_{i}}\mathbb{L}(x)\cdot\int_{D_{j}}\bm{\Upsilon}^{(k)}(x,y)\,\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{j})(y)\,dy\,dx=-k^{2}\int_{D_{i}}\mathbb{L}(x)\cdot\int_{D_{j}}\mathcal{G}^{(k)}(x,y)(y)\,dy.

Thus, the equation becomes

\displaystyle\int_{D_{i}}\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{i})(y)\ dy-\eta\sum_{j\neq i}^{M}\int_{D_{i}}\mathbb{L}(x)\cdot

\displaystyle\int_{D_{j}}\bm{\Upsilon}^{(k)}(x,y)\ \overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{j})(y)dydx=\int_{D_{i}}\mathbb{L}(x)\cdot\mathrm{E}^{\textit{in}}(x)dx

\displaystyle+k^{2}\eta\sum_{j\neq i}^{M}\int_{D_{i}}\mathbb{L}(x)\cdot\int_{D_{j}}\mathcal{G}^{(k)}(x,y)\ \overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{j})(y)dydx

(3.21)

To analyze this system, we perform a Taylor expansion of the Green’s function
\bm{\Upsilon}^{(k)}(x,y)
and
\mathcal{G}^{(k)}(x,y)
around the points
z_{i}\in D_{i}
and
z_{j}\in D_{j}
, with
i\neq j
. For
\bm{\Upsilon}^{(k)}(x,y)
, we have

\bm{\Upsilon}^{(k)}(x,y)=\bm{\Upsilon}^{(k)}(z_{i},z_{j})+\int_{0}^{1}\underset{x}{\nabla}\bm{\Upsilon}^{(k)}(z_{i}+\theta(x-z_{i}),z_{j})\cdot(x-z_{i})\,d\theta+\int_{0}^{1}\underset{y}{\nabla}\bm{\Upsilon}^{(k)}(z_{i},z_{j}+\theta(y-z_{j}))\cdot(y-z_{j})\,d\theta.

Similarly, the Taylor expansion for
\mathcal{G}^{(k)}(x,y)
is

\mathcal{G}^{(k)}(x,y)=\mathcal{G}^{(k)}(x,z_{j})+\underset{y}{\nabla}\mathcal{G}^{(k)}(x,z_{j})\cdot(y-z_{j})+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}(y-z_{j})^{\perp}\cdot\underset{y}{\textit{Hess}}\mathcal{G}^{(k)}(z_{i},z_{j}+\theta(x-z_{j}))\cdot(y-z_{j})\,d\theta.

Using the fact that
\displaystyle\int_{D_{j}}\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{j})(y)dy=0
, we can deduce the following expression from (
3.21
)

\displaystyle\int_{D_{i}}\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{i})(y)\,dy-\eta\sum_{j\neq i}^{M}\int_{D_{i}}\mathbb{L}(x)\cdot\bm{\Upsilon}^{(k)}(z_{i},z_{j})\int_{D_{j}}\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{j})(y)\,dy

\displaystyle=\int_{D_{i}}\mathbb{L}(x)\cdot\mathrm{E}^{\textit{in}}(x)\,dx+\eta\sum_{j\neq i}^{M}\int_{D_{i}}\mathbb{L}(x)\cdot\int_{0}^{1}\underset{x}{\nabla}\bm{\Upsilon}^{(k)}(z_{i}+\theta(x-z_{i}),z_{j})\cdot(x-z_{i})\,d\theta\int_{D_{j}}\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{j})(y)\,dy\,dx

\displaystyle+\eta\sum_{j\neq i}^{M}\int_{D_{i}}\mathbb{L}(x)\cdot\int_{D_{j}}\int_{0}^{1}\underset{y}{\nabla}\bm{\Upsilon}^{(k)}(z_{i},z_{j}+\theta(x-z_{j}))\cdot(y-z_{j})\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{j})(y)\,d\theta\,dy\,dx

\displaystyle+k^{2}\eta\sum_{j\neq i}^{M}\int_{D_{i}}\mathbb{L}(x)\cdot\int_{D_{j}}\underset{x}{\nabla}\mathcal{G}^{(k)}(x,z_{j})\cdot(y-z_{j})\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{j})(y)\,dy\,dx

\displaystyle+k^{2}\eta\sum_{j\neq i}^{M}\int_{D_{i}}\mathbb{L}(x)\cdot\int_{D_{j}}\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}(y-z_{j})^{\perp}\cdot\underset{y}{\nabla}\mathcal{G}^{(k)}(z_{i},z_{j}+\theta(x-z_{j}))\cdot(y-z_{j})\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{j})(y)\,d\theta\,dy\,dx.

This can be rewritten by focusing on the leading order term

\displaystyle\int_{D_{i}}\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{i})(y)\,dy-\eta\sum_{j\neq i}^{M}\int_{D_{i}}\mathbb{L}(x)\cdot\bm{\Upsilon}^{(k)}(z_{i},z_{j})\int_{D_{j}}\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{j})(y)\,dy=\int_{D_{i}}\mathbb{L}(x)\cdot\mathrm{E}^{\textit{in}}(x)\,dx+\textit{Err}_{\mathbb{L},D_{i}},

(3.22)

where

\displaystyle\textit{Err}_{\mathbb{L},D_{i}}:=\textit{Err}_{\mathbb{L},1,D_{i}}+\textit{Err}_{\mathbb{L},2,D_{i}}+\textit{Err}_{\mathbb{L},3,D_{i}}+\textit{Err}_{\mathbb{L},4,D_{i}}.

(3.23)

The following estimates for the error terms are then obtained. First, we have

\displaystyle\big|\textit{Err}_{\mathbb{L},1,D_{i}}\big|

\displaystyle:=\Big|\eta\sum_{j\neq i}^{M}\int_{D_{i}}\mathbb{L}(x)\cdot\int_{0}^{1}\nabla_{x}\bm{\Upsilon}^{(k)}(z_{i}+\theta(x-z_{i}),z_{j})\cdot(x-z_{i})\,d\theta\int_{D_{j}}\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{j})(y)\,dy\,dx\Big|

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\mathbb{L}\|_{L^{2}(D_{i})}\sum_{j\neq i}^{M}\Big\|\int_{0}^{1}\nabla_{x}\bm{\Upsilon}^{(k)}(z_{i}+\theta(x-z_{i}),z_{j})\cdot(x-z_{i})\,d\theta\Big\|_{L^{2}(D_{i}\times D_{j})}\|\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{j})(y)\|_{L^{2}(D_{j})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\delta^{4}\|\mathbb{L}\|_{L^{2}(D_{i})}\sum_{j\neq i}^{M}d_{ij}^{-4}\|\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{j})\|_{L^{2}(D_{j})}=\mathcal{O}\Big(\|\mathbb{L}\|_{L^{2}(D_{i})}\delta^{4}d^{-4}\underset{j}{\max}\|\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{j})\|_{L^{2}(D_{j})}\Big).

(3.24)

Similarly, we obtain the following for the error term

\displaystyle\big|\textit{Err}_{\mathbb{L},2,D_{i}}\big|

\displaystyle:=\Big|\eta\sum_{j\neq i}^{M}\int_{D_{i}}\mathbb{L}(x)\cdot\int_{D_{j}}\int_{0}^{1}\nabla_{y}\bm{\Upsilon}^{(k)}(z_{i},z_{j}+\theta(x-z_{j}))\cdot(y-z_{j})\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{j})(y)\,d\theta\,dy\,dx\Big|

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\Big(\|\mathbb{L}\|_{L^{2}(D_{i})}\delta^{4}d^{-4}\underset{j}{\max}\|\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{j})\|_{L^{2}(D_{j})}\Big).

(3.25)

Next, we deduce

\displaystyle\big|\textit{Err}_{\mathbb{L},3,D_{i}}\big|

\displaystyle:=\Big|k^{2}\eta\sum_{j\neq i}^{M}\int_{D_{i}}\mathbb{L}(x)\cdot\int_{D_{j}}\nabla_{x}\mathcal{G}^{(k)}(x,z_{j})\cdot(y-z_{j})\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{j})(y)\,dy\,dx\Big|

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\mathbb{L}\|_{L^{2}(D_{i})}\sum_{j\neq i}^{M}\Big\|\int_{D_{j}}\nabla_{x}\mathcal{G}^{(k)}(x,z_{j})\cdot(y-z_{j})\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{j})(y)\,dy\Big\|_{L^{2}(D_{i})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\delta^{4}\|\mathbb{L}\|_{L^{2}(D_{i})}\sum_{j\neq i}^{M}d_{ij}^{-2}\|\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{j})\|_{L^{2}(D_{j})}=\mathcal{O}\Big(\|\mathbb{L}\|_{L^{2}(D_{i})}\delta^{4}d^{-3}\underset{j}{\max}\|\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{j})\|_{L^{2}(D_{j})}\Big),

(3.26)

and finally, we have

\displaystyle\big|\textit{Err}_{\mathbb{L},4,D_{i}}\big|

\displaystyle:=\Big|k^{2}\eta\sum_{j\neq i}^{M}\int_{D_{i}}\mathbb{L}(x)\cdot\int_{D_{j}}\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}(y-z_{j})^{\perp}\cdot\underset{x}{\textit{Hess}}\mathcal{G}^{(k)}(z_{i},z_{j}+\theta(x-z_{j}))\cdot(y-z_{j})\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{j})(y)\,d\theta\,dy\,dx\Big|

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\mathbb{L}\|_{L^{2}(D_{i})}\sum_{j\neq i}^{M}\Big\|\int_{D_{j}}\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}(y-z_{j})^{\perp}\cdot\underset{x}{\textit{Hess}}\mathcal{G}^{(k)}(z_{i},z_{j}+\theta(x-z_{j}))\cdot(y-z_{j})\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{j})(y)\,d\theta\,dy\Big\|_{L^{2}(D_{i})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\delta^{5}\|\mathbb{L}\|_{L^{2}(D_{i})}\sum_{j\neq i}^{M}d_{ij}^{-3}\|\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{j})\|_{L^{2}(D_{j})}=\mathcal{O}\Big(\|\mathbb{L}\|_{L^{2}(D_{i})}\delta^{5}|\log(d)|d^{-3}\underset{j}{\max}\|\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{j})\|_{L^{2}(D_{j})}\Big).

(3.27)

Therefore, plugging the estimates (
3.24
), (
3.25
), (
3.26
), and (
3.27
) in (
3.22
), we arrive at

\displaystyle\int_{D_{i}}\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{i})(y)\ dy

\displaystyle+\eta\sum_{j\neq i}^{M}\int_{D_{i}}\mathbb{L}(x)dx\cdot\bm{\Upsilon}^{(k)}(z_{i},z_{j})\int_{D_{j}}\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{j})(y)dy=\int_{D_{i}}\mathbb{L}(x)\cdot\mathrm{E}^{\textit{in}}(x)dx

\displaystyle+\mathcal{O}\Big(\|\mathbb{L}\|_{L^{2}(D_{i})}\delta^{4}d^{-4}\underset{j}{\max}\|\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{j})\|_{L^{2}(D_{j})}\Big)+\mathcal{O}\Big(\|\mathbb{L}\|_{L^{2}(D_{i})}\delta^{4}d^{-3}\underset{j}{\max}\|\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{j})\|_{L^{2}(D_{j})}\Big)

\displaystyle+\mathcal{O}\Big(\|\mathbb{L}\|_{L^{2}(D_{i})}\delta^{5}|\log(d)|d^{-3}\underset{j}{\max}\|\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{j})\|_{L^{2}(D_{j})}\Big).

(3.28)

We now introduce the notation
\displaystyle\mathbcal{P}_{D_{i}}:=\int_{D_{i}}\mathbb{L}(x)dx.
By applying Taylor’s series expansion for
z_{i}\in D_{i},
we obtain the following expression

\displaystyle\int_{D_{i}}\mathbb{L}(x)\cdot\mathrm{E}^{\textit{in}}(x)dx

\displaystyle=\mathrm{E}^{\textit{in}}(z_{i})\cdot\mathbcal{P}_{D_{i}}+\int_{D_{i}}\mathbb{L}(x)\cdot\int_{0}^{1}\underset{x}{\nabla}\mathrm{E}^{\textit{in}}(z_{i}+\theta(x-z_{i}))\cdot(x-z_{i})d\theta

\displaystyle=\mathrm{E}^{\textit{in}}(z_{i})\cdot\mathbcal{P}_{D_{i}}+\mathcal{O}\Big(\delta^{\frac{5}{2}}\|\mathbb{L}\|_{L^{2}(D_{i})}\Big).

(3.29)

Using the expression derived above, we can further deduce

\displaystyle\int_{D_{i}}\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{i})(y)\ dy

\displaystyle-\eta\sum_{j\neq i}^{M}\mathbcal{P}_{D_{i}}\cdot\bm{\Upsilon}^{(k)}(z_{i},z_{j})\int_{D_{j}}\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{j})(y)dy=\mathbcal{P}_{D_{i}}\cdot\mathrm{E}^{\textit{in}}(z_{i})

\displaystyle+\mathcal{O}\Big(\|\mathbb{L}\|_{L^{2}(D_{i})}\delta^{4}d^{-4}\underset{j}{\max}\|\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{j})\|_{L^{2}(D_{j})}\Big)+\mathcal{O}\Big(\|\mathbb{L}\|_{L^{2}(D_{i})}\delta^{4}d^{-2}\underset{j}{\max}\|\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{j})\|_{L^{2}(D_{j})}\Big)

\displaystyle+\mathcal{O}\Big(\|\mathbb{L}\|_{L^{2}(D_{i})}\delta^{5}|\log(d)|d^{-3}\underset{j}{\max}\|\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{j})\|_{L^{2}(D_{j})}\Big)+\mathcal{O}\Big(\delta^{\frac{5}{2}}\|\mathbb{L}\|_{L^{2}(D_{i})}\Big).

(3.30)

From this point onward, we will focus on estimating the term
\mathbcal{P}_{D_{i}}
as well as determining
\|\mathbb{L}\|_{L^{2}(D_{i})}.
Initially, we note that

\displaystyle\mathbcal{P}_{D_{i}}:=\delta^{3}\int_{B_{i}}\tilde{\mathbb{L}}(\xi)d\xi=\delta^{3}\sum_{n}\Big\langle\tilde{\mathbb{L}};e^{(3)}_{n}\Big\rangle\otimes\int_{B_{i}}e^{(3)}_{n}(\xi)d\xi.

(3.31)

First, we see that

\displaystyle\int_{B_{i}}\tilde{e}^{(3)}_{n}(\xi)d\xi=\int_{B_{i}}\mathrm{I}\cdot\tilde{e}^{(3)}_{n}(\xi)d\xi=\int_{B_{i}}\mathbb{T}_{-k\delta}(\tilde{\mathbb{L}})(\xi)\cdot\tilde{e}^{(3)}_{n}(\xi)d\xi=\int_{B_{i}}\tilde{\mathbb{L}}(\xi)\cdot\mathbb{T}_{k\delta}(\tilde{e}^{(3)}_{n})(\xi)d\xi

(3.32)

Moreover, due to the definition of
\mathbb{T}_{k\delta}:=\mathbb{I}+\eta\,\mathbb{M}_{B_{i}}^{(k\delta)}+k^{2}\eta\mathbb{N}_{B_{i}}^{(k\delta)}
and the fact that the Magnetization operator
\mathbb{M}^{(0)}_{\mathrm{B}_{i}}:\nabla\mathbb{H}_{\textit{arm}}\rightarrow\nabla\mathbb{H}_{\textit{arm}}
induces a complete orthonormal basis namely
\big(\lambda^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}},\tilde{e}^{(3)}_{\mathrm{n}}\big)_{\mathrm{n}\in\mathbb{N}}
, we observe that

\displaystyle\Big\langle\tilde{\mathbb{L}};e^{(3)}_{n}\Big\rangle=\frac{1}{1+\eta\lambda_{n}^{(3)}}\int_{B_{i}}e^{(3)}_{n}(\xi)d\xi+\frac{\eta}{1+\eta\lambda_{n}^{(3)}}\Big\langle\tilde{\mathbb{L}},\widetilde{\textit{err}}_{1,\textit{al}}(e^{(3)}_{n})\Big\rangle+\frac{\eta}{1+\eta\lambda_{n}^{(3)}}\Big\langle\tilde{\mathbb{L}},\widetilde{\textit{err}}_{2,\textit{al}}(e^{(3)}_{n})\Big\rangle,

from this, we can deduce that

\displaystyle\mathbcal{P}_{D_{i}}

\displaystyle=\delta^{3}\sum_{n}\frac{1}{1+\eta\lambda_{n}^{(3)}}\int_{B_{i}}e^{(3)}_{n}(\xi)d\xi\otimes\int_{B_{i}}e^{(3)}_{n}(\xi)d\xi
